STORIES OF THE WAR.

A GUARD'S REFLECTIONS.

Conscientious Objectors And now To

Strafe Them.

Mr. Frank Kay, a member of "The Examiner" reporting staff, now on active ser- vice in Egypt, writes:— Whilst all are keen on returning for the primary reason that England is home and that therein lies, their whole source of happiness, there are others who have various secondary reasons for desiring an early return as evidence a guard I met the other day who wants to get home to " strafe " a certain conscientious objector. I cannot help but say, after he had told his story th,at I sympathised with him, for he appeared to have a very strong case. Or course, opinions diger, so I will relate the story as I got it. I was sitting in a matting and lath hut which we use as an office, trying to catch the mail, which went out the next day, with a letter to well, never mind. It was very neces- sary to save myself from a strafing that I-should catch that mail. I was writing by ,candle- light and had just written a few lines when 1 heard' the sound of a' rifle being brought to the ground from the "slope." " Only the guard having a rest," I said to myself,' and -was pre- paring to continue my letter when the guard accosted me in the familiar fashion so peculiar to all out here, "Well, how's she go?" "Not so bad," I retorted, and being eager to catch that mail I added as a hint for him to leave me alone, "I'm just writing a, letter for to-morrow's mail." That did it. I couldn't have said a worse thing, for it, gave him' the cue which he evidently wanted to enable him to relieve him- self of something which was troubling him very

much. "Mail!" he ejaculated. "Yes, and I'm goin' to catch that mail, too," he continued, "when I'm relieved of this job." 'Are you married?" he next blurted out in broad Lancashire dialect. The question asked in such a peremptory fashion without anything having happened to lead up to it, made me laugh as I replied in the affirmative, and asked good h.umcruredly, "Why do you want to know?" " Between My Girl And Me."

"Well," he said, " Yrou'll quite understand my feelings then when I tell you about an affair between my girl and me, well not me, but one o' them ` conscious ' objectors. What's thiak about them fellas? Never ow t to breathe pure air o' England." I motioned impatiently to my letter, but the hint was wasted. My acquaintance was just warming to his subject and, not being a regular "Tommy," I couldn't find the heart to tell him to go to , well, a place not unlike Egypt for heat; so, with a sigh, I meekly submitted to another spasm, hoping in my heart that very, very soon the sound of someone approaching would take him to his duty to give the chal- lenge. But Fate was unkind to me, no one came. .

Leaning on his rifle, his eyes glistening with enthusiasm and incidentally reading the only two lines of letter I had written—and the letter waAn't to a fellow soldier—he settled down to his story. "It's like this" he continued. "I've been out here and Gallipolly ' for nigh on eighteen month and me and the girl 'as corresponded reglar'. Now her mother is up agen me and doesn't take a fancy to me, so if awt goes wrong she allus makes th' most of it agen M 3. Well, we were up in line for a spell and I missed several mails. Not my fault you know, but anyhow it upset my girl. I suppose her mother had a lot to. do wi' it, and she wrote me a tell- in' off letter. Natural like as I'd bin feighten and couldn't write I were fair raw and vexed. Lads thowt nr gone mad wi' way I cursed. Anyhow, I wrote next day and lowd my girl if she thowt one o' them;' conscience objectors could do better nor me who'd better hey him. I waited for months for a reply to that letter, and at last it come by t' last mail and, such a letter."

The mention of letter again reminded me of my obligation and I dipped my pen in the ink- pot viciously. This hint did not-go unobserved, but it did not have the desired effect, for With another shuffle to get a more comfortable position against the rifle the guard excused his interruption by saying " I'se not be long and then you can finish your letter, but I want to get this off my chest." •

"This letter,"- he continued, "is a corker. It appears that some bloke from my town who was at Gallipolly ' has had leave home, and my girl's mother saw him. However he got home I don't know. I know well I 'can't get home and I've tried to 'swing it with varicose veins, but no use. Well, here my girl tells me she had a tiff with her mother and left home. She says that in a row she told her mother that I'd said she'd better hey a f con- scious ' objector, and it appears her mother re- plied, 'Well, perhaps one o' them would do thee more good nor yon neglectful soldier who goes galavantin' round Turkey. and Egypt wi' all their harems and sich like, and never tries to get a pass home to see thee." I laughed, but my guard was quite serious. Suggested Treatment For Objectors.

"Hard luck," I saki; by way of consolation. "Hard luck," he sneered contemptuously. " think it's do with ' conscious ' objectors—you know them as wants no combatant work?"

I told him I'd never considered the matter, being content to leave fools, hypocrites and cowards to their own combats in the battle of life. But he'd thought of punishments for them both quaint and humorous.

"I -wish I were a platoon commander and

could get one of them objector fellas under me. I'd give him a pair of wire cutters and give him a leg over t' parapet tellin' him to go and cut th' enemy's barbed wire. That would be non-combatant work, but I'll bet that chap would soon be back and say 'Give us a rifle quick.' " "But to put them on cutting trench barbed wire would be combatant work," I said to rectify his error. Quite erroneously the guard jumped to the conclusion that I was not at one with him in his condemnation of those who have a conscientious objection to fighting for the country in wllich they are content to live, and of which they enjoy the privileges of free- dom and equitable justice. "Well," he stammered, as he put his rifle to I his shoulder and prepared to go his rounds, "I'd I get 'era in a front line trench and put their I grub about three yards beyond the parapet to- wards the enemy lines and forbid anyone but them to fetch it. And I'd keep 'em like that until their stomach, which they can feel, beat their conscience, which they can neither feel nor see. I think they'd say 'Give us our grub, we'll tek up a rifle." I *should have liked very much to argue with him as to whether a man can feel his conscience or 'not; bet he was making for the entrance and I—well, I had my letter to finish. I will not add any morei personal comments on such a controversial subject, for I am quite satisfied with the knowledge that I, at any rate, am doing my bit. And when all is over I shall feel happier no doubt than the man who in- curred the guard's anger.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
ME FIGHTING,

As It Is Seen By The Men In The

Ranks.

Writing home about a week before he was killed, CorPorai J. E. Hill, of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, 44, John-street, describes how his regiment went to the attack. He was wounded, retunied to the Front, and was killed. "You will see," he wrote, "I have been wounded, and I know you are getting a little anxious. Cheer up, it is only slight, although it could have been very serious.. You see our division have been doing some hard fighting the last week, and you can consider that I have been very lucky. Since leaving the trenches last we have caught it pretty hot, doing noth- ing but long night marches, and by the time we got to the division's fighting sector it was time for us to go into action. We did, too. You see a division had captured their objective and we had to go for ours the next morning, which was a.great wood. Well, everything went on all right that night, and we got relieved by the other half of our brigade, who were making a charge, the following -morning, and with us taking the line over our battalion was put in reserve. We had hardly got to reserve 'when we were called back again. We got there, and my word, what a surprise we had. We got sorted out for the third attempt, and it was my luck to be left-hand section of the rear line. We had 15ayonets fixed and every- thing was ready, when the Adjutant came and we got the order to unfix bayonets. The charge was cancelled and, my word, I was glad. Many times I prayed to get out of it safe.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans Bayoneted Our Wounded.

"By this time night had come on, and our stretcher-bearers, who, were collecting wounded, say that they saw the Germans come out of the wood and start bayoneting our wounded. Well, that made my blood run cold. Often I wished then we had gone over and stuck the lot of them. I don't suppose you will hear about that bit of German work. The reason the thing was cancelled wfis that plans had been rearranged and, of course, we went back to _reserve for a short rest. From our reserve we could see all that was going on in the front line. I saw a division go over, and, my word, what a sight it was. I Ichow you will read about the steadiness of our troops, but you can't realise—seeing is believing—and, my word, they were fine, no running and going mad over the thing. They walked quite calmly. I can't tell you how they did go. It was fine, and to make things better our artillery played 'havoc with the German position. While watching this I did not think for one moment that our division were attack- ing almost at the same time, and with our brigade having sá many casualties we were reserve brigade for the division. Well, things went on fine all that day, and we were not called upon until 2 o'clock the following morning. We got up the line about six. Of course, we went all round for the sake of dodging the shells, and it took us all that time to get. about 500 yards. We assembled in a deep valley to form up into artillery formation, as shells were dropping like hail. One company got over the ridge all right, and, of course, it came to our turn. We divided into sections, and when I got up on to to see when our best time to go was, a 'coal box' came about 20 yards from me, and, of course, a piece about the size of an egg landed me full bang in the left kidney. Down I went with a bang, and I remembergd nothing until I found myself all of a heap at the bottom oi the ridge. My word,,talk about Joe Wads- worth hitting me in the kidneys, it was not in it, I thought a steam hammer had caught me. Luckily enough, ,it only made a slight bruise, but it is the shock I am suffering from. That even is not much, but take it from me, I um having a good rest ,and four' meals a day. It seemed a treat to see bread here after six days on biscuits and bully. While here I have met plenty of ,the boys out of my f3latoon, and, ray word, what a tale ,they had. They got to the wood all right. They say it is worse than Loos. You see the Germans had it like a palace. I The prisoners, say that , they go there from Verdun on rest, in fact in one dug-out there was a piano and plenty of grub—don't say the Germans are short. Anyhow, when our boys got there they had to quit and be smart about it, too.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans Surrendering.

"You ought to see the prisoners giving them- selves up, old men and young lads, too, but mind you, the size of them is wonderful, and Fü is the 'stupidness of them. I believed , nearly 100 of them gave _themselves up' to _six chaps. _ _

"Our lot meant to take that woo il and take it they did in fine style, but at what a cost:"

===== 0002601_19160930_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Butter or Margarine? At last week's meeting of the Board of Guardians, Mr. W. J. Shaw seemed anxious that the inmates of the Workhouse should be supplied with butter inffead of _margarine, and he alleged that existing conditions caused uneasiness outside. I suppose it will hardly be pretended that the consumption of margarine •causits uneasiness inside. What, quality of butter 'Would Mr. Shaw and his friends sub- stitute for the really good margarine now used? I have a relative who has for many years been sister in a Yorkshire Union Hos- pital, and she longs for the very change that has evidently lead to such heartsearching on the part of Mr. Shaw and some others. She says the strong butter now served in the insti- tution is simply horrible, and all concerned would greatly prefer margarine—the lingering misgiving :as to which in some quarters is due to unreasoning preiudice.

The 'Mayoralty. - We are all glad to be assured that Alderman Peter Peacock is to continue Mayor of War- rington -for 'a foid.th year. Apart from personal considerations, one could not at present con- temPlate with equanimity the possibility of supercession in the exalted ;office which the woithy "holder has so admirably 'filled and adorned over a term already equal to that for Which his most honoured predecessors have served. Thus his Worships • experience is destined to ;constitute a record. The -case is somewhat similar to that of an East Lanca- shire borough in which I am ,nrerestetl, having formerly spent some years there. Eight along from -incorporation to the time When I 'left, it I appeared to have been the rule for the Mayor for the time being to serve for three years, but I I learn that it was broken owing to the war, and that although in ordinary circumstances the Chief Magistrate there in 1914 would have retired toward the end of that year, his Wor- ship still holds the reins of office, and is to be re-elected for a further twelve- months. An Awkward Situation.

Years ago I lived in a town the exceedingly' wealthy Mayor of which evidently enjoyed the position and work very much. He had been at the head of affairs for several years, and other aspirants for succession thereto, while they could not openly rebel, were known to chafe under the embarrassing state of things. At this period each year, out of courtesy and pur- suant to local custom, the Council formally in- vited his Worship to serve as Mayor for yet another year, and the distinguished one, flat-' tered by the insincere speeches made i]r• his praise time after time, 1/4graciously consented— to the grOwing despair and disgust of prospec- tive candidates for the coveted seat of honour.

Not of Local Application. Let me hasten to guard against misappre- hension here—merely as a precaution, that is. The case just referred to is not for a moment comparable with that of Warrington or of the other place in question, nor is the implied criticism in any sense applicable to these. That, of course, 99 per cent. of my readers will quite understand, but it shopld be clear to all. It will be equally plain to everyone that in normal times it is an excellent plan to ensure the regular circulation of these municipal honours. It is pleasant to think that there is a practically exhaustless store of patriotic energy in our citizenship; but, of course, if men of education and ability are to be expected to bear their part freely and disinterestedly in the great field of public service, we must always be pre- pared to recognise the claim of any eligible aspirant for the highest honour the community has at disposal—nay, I would rather urge that we must ever continue to be so prepared, for our representatives have hitherto been so guided and I at least perceive no signs ot deterioration in that respect. The Mayor-elect may be confidently assured of the cordial and sincere congratulations and the grateful thanks of the burgesses. Gratitude, you know, has been wittily defined as "a lively sense of favours to come," and in this particular in- stance the prevailing sentiment in relation to Alderman 'Peacock may certainly be so inter- preted.

• Arresting Headlines. " Vigilant " has a' happy facility in coining these; as noted in last week's " Gossip," his "Might of the Mite" has carried his fame to the Metropolis. Alone he did it. I happen to have done likewise into the teens of years ago, though I was too slow—or may I say too ex- cessively modest (a-hem !)—to take the small prize to which my achrevement entitled me. I had been appointed to a little town where it was customary to abstain from street lighting three days before and three days after the date markecl, on the almanac as "full moon." But Phceba often failed to keep her \ tryst; then darkness covered the face of that towri and gross ,darkness the people. At length sundry and divers bold and enterprising spirits on the Town Council determined, in a reckless, bang- went-saxpence sort of-way, that the public lamps of that theretofore benighted borough should be nightly lit continuously throughout the winter months Now to my point: one of the head- lines of my report of the memorable proceedings ran, "Dissolving partnership With th 6 Moon," and a smart inhabitant of those) parts lost no time in sending the example to a periodical offering prizes for such things, and was awarded a guinea fpr it. "Alone hel did it"; yes, in- deedy, as Sam Slick was wont to express it.

Hard Lines, But That gallant member of the " Examiner " staff, Mr. Frank Kay, who has go long served with the Colours without holiday, has his own characteristic serio-comic way of commenting upon the situation. As he well knows,, there are many to share his grievance, and at the same time join in thundering an emphatic denial that they are in the least downhearted. One of my three soldier-nephews enlisted in the R.F.A. immediately on th 4 outbreak of war, and a very few months' later was actively engaged on the Western Front. At last he is fain to confess that he would like a week's leave or so, but—doubtless for good and sufficient reasons—he has been unable trrobtain it. An old young colleague of mine has covered 20,000 miles as a dispatch rider in Northern France, and, needless to state, has had various adven- tures and narrow escapes. No wonder he, too, begins to feel a little homesick, but leave is in his case also out of the question. Aeronautics.

A friend of mine Anvites us to imagine Mat Prior—minor poet as well as politician and diplomatist, of the age of Queen Anne (since deceased, according to eminent authorities' repeated assurance) and of the "wee, wee German lairdie " of the Scots Jacobite ballad (George I.)--,imagine Mat Prior„ in the "silver age" of English literature, sitting over a pipe and a glass of home-brewed ale with Pope and Swift, Addison and Steele. One of the company declares that he has been the subject of a supernatural communication. In a vision it ba,,• been revealed to him that, in less- than 200 years ahead, men will fly like birds through the air; that the flight of birds, then only in a figurative sense to be imitated by man, would be actually emulated in the air by men and women. Mat Prior and the wits around him then would have heaped scorn _and derision upon such a dreamer. But the dream has come true. The conquest of the air is not only well begun, but it is much more 'than half done. The Farman Biplane.

These reflections were: induced as on Satur- day afternoon I gazed skyward and watched the graceful flight of a Farman biplane. I am At the end of my tether, and may not dwell on the matter, but perhaps I may just be allowed to repeat one of the favowite stories of Mr. Orville Wright, the fanious flying man. It is a story about a man who called at a biplane selling agency and said: "Mt older brother bought a biplane here last month. You told him if any- thing broke you'd supply the missing parts." "Yes. Well?" said the manager. "Well," said the caller, "my brother wants you to supply him right away with six new ribs, a left leg, half a dozen assorted finger, and a medium-sized Roman hose."

THE LATCHFORD CADET CORPS.

Orders for week ending October 6th, 1916:— Parades.—Tuesday Parades are suspended until further notice. Friday: Fall in at 7.30 prompt, Company Drill, &c.

Orderlies of the week.—Corporal E. Lea and Lance-Corporal Ashton.

J. B. STARKEY, Commanding Officer.

RIFLEMAN W. H. HOWARD.

ounded Again Mille Mending A Captured German Trench.

Rifleman William H. Howard, son of Mr. and Mrs. A. Howard, 36, Orford-avenue, has been wounded for the second time. Writing frpm a French hospital, he says: "I have been wounded again, but not quite so seriously as before. I stopped a piece of shrapnel last Friday, the 15th, in the big push,' when our battalion went over the top. I managed to get to the place which we had to take. We were trying to get the trench a little bit stronger, when I got wounded. It hit my chin and then my throat, and it was a good job it did catch my chin first as it stopped the force of it. I was attended by a doctor as soon as I was hit, and if he hadn't been there I would have been 'landowner' by now. As soon as the Germans quietened down with the shelling I got out of the way as soon as possible. The Germans are a lot of cowards. They fight as long as you like until we get, on top of them, and then they throw their rifles down and cry for mercy. After any of the German wounded are left be-

RIFLEMAN W. H. HOWARD. Wounded. ~

Wakefield, South Lancashire Regiment, has been wounded. On Wednesday his parents went. to see him in hospital. His pal, Private Harold Smart, who resided next door, was also wounded. Private Wakefield, who is the elder son, was gassed in June of last year: He is 22 years —of age, and was formerly. employed by Messrs. A. Waring and Co., Ltd., tanrers, Winwiek-road.

Private Appleton, who is nineteen years of age, and was educated. at Beamont School, was formerly employed in the hairdressing estab- lishment of Mr. Freeman, Friars' Green. He enlisted almost eleven mOnths ago, and had been at the Front ten weeks. He worshipped at the Wycliffe Congregational Church, and played cricket with the Wycliffe team. His father, Mr. John Appleton, is in Scotland with the National Reserve.

MISSING.

RIFLEMAN R. UNSWORTH.

War Office Say " Missing" ;

Comrades " He Is Dead."

Official news has reached Mrs. Unsworth, 55, Hardy-street, that her husband, Rifleman Robert Unsworth, of the K.R.R., has been miss- ing since August 24th. In addition to the official news, a letter has reachell Warrington from a local man 'famed Hindes: It appears that the latter and Rifleman' Unsworth made an agreement that if anything happened to either of them the other should write home and con- vey the news. This Hindes has done; and in a letter to his wife he says he inquired of three men belonging to the same regiment as Rifle-

son, Private Joseph Jones,,of the K. 0.5.8., has been posted as missing after ay_ engagement on September 3rd. Official news to this effect has been received.

Private Jones, who is twenty-seven years of age, enlisted about twelve months ago, and had been at the Front ten months. He was edu- cated at Holy Trinity School, and was formerly employed by /Messrs. John Melbourne, Ltd.

IN THE COURTS.

ASSAULTS ON POLICEMAN.

Magistrates Disagree As To Punishment Deserved.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor , (in the chair), Aldermen Bolton, Smethurst and James Evans, and Messrs. S. Wright, W. Furness, G. Pilling and T. Locker, A man of the labouring class named John Bartley, no fixed abode, pleaded- guilty when charged with being drunk and disorderly in Barbauld-street on Saturday night, and with assaulting Special Inspector Mason. Special Inspector Mason stated that at 9.35 on Saturday evening he was in Barbauld-street visiting patrols. Prksonet, who was in a drunken condition, was shouting after two women. When he- came towards witness he acted in an 'indecent' manner and witness arrested him. Defendant then raised his right hand, clenched, and struck witness on the jaw. Witness grappled with defendant and they both went down to the ground. Having struggled for some time he managed to blow his whistle for assistance, which did not come immediately. As his right arm was pot yet sound, prisoner got away and ran towards StanleyLstreet. Wit- ness overtook him and closed with him. Another struggle ensued and prisoner succeeded in entering a lodging-house. Detectives Ander-

ton and Turner Came to his assistanct. The officers brought him out of the lodging-house and ,Special Sergeant Brad- burn and witness brought him to the Police. Station. In Museum-street prisoner again be- came violent, and it was found necessary to put on the handcuffs. Prisoner said he was sorry for what he had done. _ _ _

_ The magistrates, the Mayor announced, had failed to agree upon. a decision, and the case would be re-tried on the 'following day.

Conduct Not To Be Tolerated.

At Tuesday's Court, when prisoner was again in the dock, the magistrates were : Councillor Henry Roberts and Messrs. John Rymer Young, Thomas Locker and C. Jackson

Holmes, The Chief Constable (Mr. Nicholls) said the charge had been heard the 'previous day, but

the Bench could not agree to what sentence should be pronounced, and they adjourned the case;

- Special Inspector Mason having repeated his story, The Chief Constable said that defendant had no settled place of abode. He was a labourer, and did not belong to Warrington. On behalf of the regular police he desired to point out very strongly this assault on an officer. This man struck a Special Inspector in the face, and unless they could guarantee the support of

the Bench to support these men it was not right to those who were coming forward to ren- _ _ _

der services to the town of which it was very badly in need at the present time. If it was one of his own constables he wQuld not mind so much, but these men, who were giving their services free and so much of their time, should be protected. The Chairman said the Beneh were deter-

- mined to support the action a their Special Constables quite as strongly as they would sup- port the action of the ordinary constables. This kind of conduct would not be tolerated. Defendant was 'fined 305., or, in default, a month's imprisonment.

TOO-SPEEDY AMBULANCES

A Fine And A Warning To Hospital Motor Drivers.

It was alleged at the Borough Police Court on Thursday that the motor ambulances in connection with the Lord Derby Military Hospital, Winwick, are driven at too great a speed 'generally, and a warning was given &tir- ing a ease in which Private Thomas Livesley was charged with driving at an excessive speed and to the danger of the public. Special Inspector Richardson said that on September 12th one of his horses and carts was crossing Marson-street into Tanner's-lane, when Livesley came flying by with a motor ambulance, crashing into the horse and cart and knocking off part of the shafting. Livesley did not stop. Livesley said he was not going more than four miles an hour; he was only on second gear. The Special: He was going at from twelve to fifteen miles an hour.

A witness went even so far as to fix the speed as high as seventeen miles per hour. A Lieutenant from the hospital said that Livesley was driving some dangerous lunatics 'and dared not stop; ,moreover, he did not know he had totiched the horse and cart. The Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls): It might have been one of the lunatics who was driving. (Laughter.) The Lieutenant put Lance-Corporal Franklin, who is in charge of the motor ambulance garage, into the witness box, and that witness said that it was impossible , for a driver who was impossible for a driver who was on second gear to go seventeen miles an hour with that motor.

Chief Constable Surprised.

The Chief Constable remarked that ,he was surprised to find that the authorities of the Lord Derby War hospital supported their drivers tearing about in the way they did. It was 'a 'positive disgrace that those vehicles should proceed at so high a speed. Anybody who was about Warrington when the convoys were coming in would agree. After they had emptied they proceeded at a great rate. Only the other afternoon he saw one of them turn round Bridge 'Foot at twenty-five miles hour.

The Chairman (Mr. A. Jolley) said that ou the, evidenCe, apart from general considera- tions they considered the prosecution had proved their case. As it was the first case that had been brought before • the Coue- it would be dealt With leniently, but if any other cacei were brought the. fine. would ,he much more heavy. Fine: 205., the licence to be end-r..sel.

WORTHLESS CIGARETTES.

Londoner Defrauds Two Local Tradespeople.

Already serving a sentence for a similar offence, James McKay, of 3, Ballance-road, Homerton, London, was brought from Derby Prison and Charged at the Warrington Borough Police Court on Thursday with obtaining £2 is. 6d. and £4 Is. by false pretences from two tradespeople in different parts of the town.' Through Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, he pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable 'said that the prisoner in Septembpr of last year went to one of the prosecutors; who kept,an off-licence, and asked him to give a good display of some herbal cigarettes. Various inducements were offered, including one that his place would be made the depot for the district. He was tcld that it was usual to pay for the first elivery and if the) were unsold or he was not satisfied with them the money would he returned in a month. The cigarettes were also to be excvely adver- tit,ed in the town and district. In view of that this prosecutor gave the prisoner £2 fs. 6d. It was afterwards found that the cigarettes were worthless, and there was no, call for them. The other prosecutOr was told a similar story and paid £4 is. Until to-day the parties had not met the prisoner again. It was a most despicable fraud. Setitence: Three months' impriSonment, to run. from Thursday.

"ALL RIGHT IN WARRINGTON," BUT NOT IN RUNCORN.

At Runcorn, on Monday, James Isherwood, taxi-driver, Bedford-street, Stockton Heath, was fined 10s. for not having obscured the lights of a car which he was driving at Sutton Weaver on the night of the 14th September. Sergeant McKenna said that the defendant, upon being asked why he did not obscure the lights, replied: "They're all right in War- rington."

tztters toot Oita

CONCERTS FOR THE WOUNDED.

Sir,—l fully agree with " Amateur's " sug- gestion that some lady or gentleman should be asked 'to undertake the responsibility of organising good concerts for the wounded Tommies " during the coming winter. This Is the only opportunity some will get to do some- thing for the lads, who are doing so much for us, and I feel aure if anyone will move in the ]natter, there will be a good response from the artistes of Warrington. I have twice had the pleasure of entertaining wounded " Tommies " ' at the Infirmary, and I may safely say that I have never had a more appreciative audience. "Come again" they said. I can assure you they will feel well repaid after the season.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
SAT ON A FIRE.

Terrible Scene In A Rylands

Street Shop.

BOY'S AWFUL DISCOVERY.

Telling her young nephew that he could go out for a walk for a couple of hours on Sunday evening, and that if anything happened to her he was to inform her uncle, Mrs. Edith rieesom, a draper, of 11, Rylands-street, was found on his return to be seated on the kitchen fire, her head up the chimney, her feet dangling from the grate, and lier clothes in a biaze. The boy ran fcr a police- man, upon whose arrival she was dead, por- tions of her body being scorched away and her face charred and covered with soot. It was a terrible sight. _ _

, The inquest was held on Wednesday after- noon, and the proceedings were of a remarkable character.

THE HUSBAND'S EVIDENCE.

The husband, Charles Heesom, a general labourer, residing at the Girls' High School, nalifax, said he had not been living with hit Wife for twenty-one months, and had not seen her during that time. He had, however, corre- sponded with her and occasionally sent her small sums of money. ' He heard from her as recently as a fortnight ago, and she seemed to be all right. She had never complained of being in financial difficulties, although she said she suffered from asthma and pains in the head.' The Coroner (Mr. S. Brighouse) : Why did yo., Part ?-0n account of business. The war caused it to go down and I left her to look after it 'while I went and worked elsewhere. I was compelled to work, and did not want to do so in Warrington. " There were no domestic difficulties?-oh, no. Have you any family?—No.

So your wife stayed here to carry on the business while you went to Halifax to work?— Yes.

Was this a mutual arrangemer. between you and your wife?-oh, yes. ' She agreed that I should go on account of the business. Have you any idea why she might have taken her life ?—No.

You have not gOt the last letter she wrote you? —No. - -

_ When you left her did this little boy, a nephew—Wilfred Lyndon Heesom—go and stay With her ?—Yes. He came after I went away. Did anyone live in the house except these rwo?—A lady and gentleman lodged there at One time.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
BOY'S HORRI BLE DISCOVERY.

The boy referred to was then called. Nearly 14 years of age, he gave his evidence fluently and lucidly. He said he had lived with his aunt for three or four months and had acted as her errand boy. She had complained to him of severe pains in the head and had also suffered very much from asthma. When the pains were very bad she would go upstairs and sometimes Bay, "If anything happens tell Uncle Frank," Meaning Mr. Frank Turner, ex-superintendent of the Warrington Fire Brigade. On Sunday night she looked very pale. At seven o'clock witness asked her if he could go out for a walk.

‘` She replied that I could go for two hours," Went on the lad, "and if on my return I found anything the matter with her I was to run for Uncle Frank. I returned about a quarter to nine o'clock and on entering the shop smelt something burning. On going into the kitchen I found auntie „sitting on the top of the fire With her head up the chimney. I could just see her face. Her clothes were all on fire atm I tried to get her away, but could not. I then ran and told the policeman at Market Gate."' • P.C. Harrison said the woman was absolutely dead when he got to the scene. In a cupboard he found a note, which she had written, as follows, "Dear Wilfred,—Give -Uncle Frank the money." Mr. Turner was sitting in the well ..of the Court and Mr. T. S. Steel was present as his solicitor. The Coroner asked whether Mr. Turner could, say anything about the matter, and he replied that he could not, only to say that the lad took him the note, and on going there the police, were already on the scene. The Coroner: What' does the note mean—that she owed 'Mr. Turner some money? Mr. Steel: Yes.

The Coroner: What was the trouble between her and her husband ? Is this a true story I have heard? Mr. Steel consulted Mr. Turner and. replied, 'She never said anything about it to Mr. Turner."

===== 0002601_19160930_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CONSTABLE'S THEORY.

'turning to the Constable, the Cor'oner asked whether Mrs. Heesom could have got into the Position accidentally? "No," he rejoined. " She could not She must have deliberately sat on the fire. In my opinion she must have got .hold of the mantelpiece, edged herself up the chimney and lowered herself on to the fire."

===== 0002601_19160930_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
SOMETHING JURIES NEVER 'LEARN.

Addressing the jury, the Coroner remarked that in this kind of case there was always a great deal which they never learned. " You get to know it some time after the verdict has been s given," he continued. "Of course, there is something at the back of this. No woman Would put herself on the fire unless there was ilorne reason. We all want to live as long as We can; not one of us wants to terminate his or her life without cause." So saying, the Coroner left the mystery wfh the jury and they returned an open verdict.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUNERAL.

The funeral took place on Thursday after- -neon at the Warrington Cemetery. There .Lvere present, among inany others, Mr. Charles esom: (husband), Mr. Turner (father), Mrs. nton and Mrs. Jepson (sisters-in-law), Mrs., waitson (niece) Miss G. Hinton, Mr. Frank 'Turner (uncle), Mr. and Mrs.' Frank Turner', -Mr. Allen, Mr. Reyes, Mr. and Mrs. Carmen, Mrs. Potter, Mr. and Mrs. Cook. Wreaths and floral 'tributes were inscribed: le loving memory from her husband," With deepest sympathy, from Mrs. Hinton," ats. Jepson. Mrs. Watson. The Rev. J. G. H. Hollingworth read the service.

The funeral arrangements were carried out Daessrs. MounAeld anct.SQ4.s.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
THE YOUNG GENERATION.

Councillor R. Henshall On

Spiritual Wellbeing.

• Special meetings in ,onnection with the War- rington Sunday School Union were held in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall on Saturday afternoon and evening.

The Rev. W. Melville Harris, secretary or Young People's Department of the Congrega- tional Union, delivered two very helaul addresses.

Tea was served in the Hall, and was followed by a tea-table conference. Mr. Arthur Moun- field presided, and spoke on "The religious life of the scholar, its awakening, development and direction." The Revs. W. M. Harris and P. meson, Miss Vanda Taylor, Mr. A. Hoyle, Mr. J. Griffiths, and others also spoke. - Value of Religious Training.

Councillor R. Henshall presided at the seven o'clock meeting, which was well attended, and said there was never a period in their history when they needed to emphasise so much as no 4 the spiritual aspect of the education of the child. They were spending well over £l,OOO a week in Warrington, wisely or foolishly, oi. "training the young idea how to shoot," and so far as he was able to judge, as a member of the Education Committee, the general defini- tion of education merely meant the sharpen- ing of the intellectual qualities. The moral qualities were nearly always left out. A privilege and a solemn obligation • were laid upon every Sunday school teacher to see that a spirit of reverence—he did not mean ,merely reverence in the Conventional sense—and lovu and affection pure and undefiled, grew up in their scholars. After the war was over they, might probably spend their money less foolishly on the intellectual education of the child, but surely, there was abundant evidence that they had whollk missed the mark if they merely trained the mind and left the heart untoucheu. The only way permanently to infliAence the scholar was by the example of the teacher. The Rev. W. M. Harris took as the subject of his address, "The call of the kingdom." He urged upon Sunday school teachers the import- ance of being thorough and earnest in their work. God's call was for skilled workmen, not merely casual labourers. \Teachers must he living teachers, and they muAt ever be learners. They must themselves remain scholars right up to .the end of the chapter, And if they did they would know how to handle the young and beautiful life placed in their care in such a way that they would never brush off the bloom. They must seize the opportunity when it pre- sented itself, and if they did they would indeed become workmen "who need not be ashamed." There was no office on earth more important than that of the teacher. .(Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160930_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDREN'S SERVICE.

On Sunday afternoon a delightful united scholars' service was held in Bold-street Wes- leyan Church, this handsome building being tastefully decorated with harvest trophies, it is 'a pity that the older people did not attend in larger numbers. As it was, however, the fine gathering of children overflowed from the galleries into the body of the church. Rev. Percy Meson was the chairman, and a portion of SO Mark's Gospel was read by the Rev. C. P. Pitt. There was a special hymn sheet, and Miss Minnie Whitmore contributed a solo most effectively.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
Better,. Sweeter, Purer Day,

The speaker was Alderman Townend, of Middleton, who has an extraordinary gift in speaking to child audiences and, indeed, to grown-ups, too. Each child he saw there, said Alderman Townend, reminded him of a notice he once saw on a vacant mill—" Power—to let.'• What were those boys and girls going to do: what power had they to- let? What was the life they were going to live? They should re- member that they had come from God and that they had to go back to God and, keeping that/ fact in view, they should make up their minds and hearts to go out into the world to do some- thing for God. Out of all this stress and tur- moil of the present time he hoped to see a better, sweeter and purer time for the young generation he saw before him. He wanted each of them to go out into the world as a soldier of Jesus Christ and gave them as their motto, "Do it to-day." He told them a story of Ruskin, that great writer whose books, when they grew up, they would revel in reading. He had a white piece of marble on the desk lb his study, and on this piece of marble he had painted the one .word, "To-day."

Juvenile collectors took up the offering. The members of the Boys' Brigade attended with their band.

SWIMMING GALA.

Secondary School Students'

Prowess Rewarded.

On Wednesday afternoon the annual swim- ming gala for the students at the Secondary School took place at the Baths. The students were present in good numbers to cheer their favourites on to victory. The competitors themselves were in splendid condition, and some good swimming and diving were witnessed. The life-saving competition was excellently performed. Perhaps the item that created the most amusement was the hunting of the whistle, in which the hunters all blino- foldpd, chased by sound the elusive person with the whistle.

After the programme had been gone through, the winners were rewarded for their skill in this particular direction by the presentation ~f certificates by Mrs. Cann, who received a bouquet from Miss Lucy Lygo, the girls' cham- pion. At the close the Principal, Mr. J. S. Broome, thanked Mrs. Cann for her kindness in coming and for the way in which she had distributed the certificates. The vote was cordially passed by the students, and Mrs. Cann suitably replied.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 30, 1916.

• ••• •• •• •• • •:.• ••••• • • •••:".• ••• • ••• •• • • • • • • • • ..:••••••• • t) •:•• ••• 4••• •• • • ••• • • •• • • ••••• ••• • • ••• •••• • • • • •• • • • •• • • •• • • • • •• • • • , .:. .:. . .:.

and be indispensable for wearing over the one piece frock which is to reign supreme. The cost of one of these coats in one or other of the many imitations of short-haired pelts, is not an extravagance, be- cause afternoon frocks and all descriptions of thin dresses may be worn under them. The new coats and capes have such delightfully warm linings that one is led to infer that manu- facturers and mantle makers are anticipating a very severe winter. All the new models are very full, and the collars extravagantly high, if not cumbersome, but this will doubtless tone down as the winter comes on. Capes in fur, velvet, fur cloths and plush, will make a bid for favour also, they have been fashioned on the lines of the garment worn by the French peasant, are round in shape, very full, and have slits for the arms, and, of course, high fur collars.

High Heels and Backache. Writing on the subject of backache in the September "Good Health," Dr. A. B. Olsen gives the following warning against high heels: "High-heeled boots are sufficient to cause an unnatural position and thus bring On backache. Nature intended us to walk on the iteels.as well as the balls of the feet and raising them more than an inch above the floor .means inviting trouble. There can be no doubt but that heels are an invention of man and that natural foot- wear would_ not be provided with heels. The sandals which were the first shoes of primitive man- did not raise the heels above the rest of the foot, and this is undoubtedly the natural position in 'standing and walking. But a small departure from this rule is not more 'than an inch at the. very outside, can: be adjusted by the ligaments and. muscles of the back without causing pain: Raising the heals still higher from the ground is almost certain to cause strain and bring suffering." " Eat Less Meat."

We aro still urged by the Government to eat less meat and if people only realised the bene- fit in health, strength and vitality that woulu ensue by cutting down animal flesh to the mini- mum there would be no danger of a meat famine. There is no need of taking meat of any kind for •breakfast,for a small helping once a day provides an ample supply of the repair and building nourishment material which the chief ingredient of lean meat. When one uses fat in the form of butter or margarine little if any other fat is necessary. A breakfast eon- sisting of brown or standard bread and butter, oatmeal porridge and milk, stewed fruit such as prunes or currants or a few dates with or with- out an apple or a banana makes a wholesonw, nourishing, and well-balanced meal on which to start the work of the day. A few pine kernels or other nuts may be added if desired. Pine kernels make an excellent substitute for ham or bacon and are far superior in food value and wholesomeness.

Rice and Herb Soup. Well wash loz. of whole unpolished rice, and put it into a pint of boiling and salted water; boil for thirty to forty minutes, and tfien add half a pint of skimmed 'milk, a little salt, some parsley chopped fine, and a saltspoonful each of powdered thyme and marjoram. Boil again for five minutes, and if thicker than desired ad.l more milk or water befOre serving. Preventives of Moths.

"CARRY ON."

Watchword Of The Musical Society. •

A SEASON'S PROGRAMME.

"Carry on" is the watchword of the War- rington Musical Society, which held its annual meeting in the Cairo-street Schoolroom on Tues- day evening, following a practice by the chorus and orchestra.

That it has justified its existence during the war may be judged from the facts that while it has fostered and maintained the art of music in .the borough, last year it was the means of

handing over t 34 13s. Bd. to the Prisoners of War Fund. In all it has contributed nearly £lOO to war funds.

Of cOurse, the Society has its difficulties— those of an adverse balance of £l6 13s. 9d., and a scarcity of male voices, but it is hoped that these will be improved. Mr. W. E. At-kin, a vice-president and past secretary of the Society, presided over the meet- ing. _

'Submitting his report, the Hon. Secretary, Mr. Lionel Fairclough, referred with great regret to the death of Mh,Patrick Walsh, who had acted as librarian for several years. He went on to say that in spite of any drawbacks they could congratulate themselves not only on having kept the Society going, but on having given last season two 'concerts and a perform- ance of "The Messiah,", and handed a cheque to the Mayor for £34 13s. Bd. in aid '9f the Prisoners of War Fund.,,

===== 0002601_19160930_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
Basses in the Army.

" Naturally, we have lost a considerable pro- portion of our male members, especially amongst the basses, and this section of the chorus, for the first time I can remember, was numerically weakv than the tenors," he added. "The season previous we had 27 basses, last season 12 only. This entails very hard work on the remainder, so much so that it was felt that unless we could get some more assistance it would be impossible to carry on. Judging by the new faces we have seen to-night, I am happy to say that I believe this difficulty has been overcome. We worked last season on very similar lines to the season before, with the f,x- ception that, by the courtesy of the Mayor, we were able to give two concerts in the Parr Hall, as against one the season previous. The Rectpr again placed the Parish Church at our disposal. We gave a performance of "The Messiah," at which a collection realised £l4 10s. 11d., which I am sorry to say was considerably short of the collection at "The Elijah" per- formance-229 10s. 7d. However, as I mentioned before, we were able to hand over the sum of £34 13s. Bd. to the Mayor, being profits on the two concerts, and the collection in the Parish Church, in aid of the Local War Prisoners' Fund. The, strength of the chorus was as fol- lows :—Sopranos 42, previous season 53, per- centage of attendances 66.66; altos 48, ,previous season 56, percentage of attendances 68.75; tenors 17,4 previous season 23, percentage of at- tendances 58.23; basses 12, previous season 27, percentage of attendances 66.66. The strength of the orchestra was 32 against 35 the season before. By the courtesy Of Mr. Cahill, the bandmaster of the South Lancashire Regiment, we ,were assisted at practices and concerts _I3Y a double bass, a horn, a trombone and two cornets from his band, so that with their assistance Our bill for professional help at the concerts was very small. I understand they would not be available this season, as the band had been transferred. We owe them our best thanks for the help the gave, and there is no doubt that their instruments and assistance at the practices made them considerably more interesting." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160930_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRESENT AND THE FUTURE.

In submitting his statement, the Hon. Treasurer, Mr. J. P. Reynolds, said that on the whole the season had not been an unsatisfactory one, although the amount handed over to the Prisoners of War Fund was rather small and a little disappointing. It might have been better. During the two war seasons the Society had handed over just shot of £lOO to the war funds, which was not a bad record. On the two seasons there was a total adverse balance of £l6 13s. 9d., £9 of which was carried forward from last season. Really that deficit should have been deducted from the profits of the con- certs, but the committee felt that, as the amount which would then be available for the Prisoners of War Fund would be so small, it was not desirable to deduct anythinr ior expenses. That, however, was not a policy that could be continued indefinitely. Members' subscriptions —234 12s. 6d.—showed a falling off, due in great measure to the loss of male members, many of whom had gone to serve their country. The concert subscriptions (X3O 35.) were not unsatiS- factory in the circumstances, but they would have been better if members had shown a 'little more enthusiasm. There was a number of members who did splendidly, but.others did little or nothing in the sale of tickets. In addition, many of their subscribers kindly gave additional subscriptions to enable the Society to defray the working expenses, and thus avoid drawing on the profits of the concerts. The sum raised in this way was £29 4s. With regard to the future that was rather a difficult 'question, and one which the committee would have to con- sider very carefully. His own feeling was that they could do nothing but "carry on" in the same way as last season. It seemed absolutely hopeless to attempt to go back to their ordinary routine or try to build up their subscription list, but they could go on and do as last season. One concert had already been arranged foi November, but that was all that had been taken in hand up to the present. A suggestion which he regarded as a very good one was that they should give The Redemption" in the Parish Church during Lent. He had mentioned the proposal to Canon Willis, who was very favourable to it. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160930_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT A BUSINESS CONCERN.

Also urging the Society to "carry on," the Chairman said that: after all, what was a loss of £1.6 when they compared it with the amount actually handed over to a most deserving fund? Of course, they had to bear in mind the present high cost of living and the fact that there were so many appeals to the public. Even if the loss on the coming season's work happened to be larger still, he did not think that, in the special circumstances, it would be any reason for discouragement. The Society had such a long continuity that he 'believed it would ,be a fatal thing to wind it up, even for one season. He implored them to keep it.going. Of course, they could not fight against the impossible, and if the war lasted longer than was anticipatei, it might become necessary to suspend operations. At the present time, mowever, he saw no reason to contemplate such a course. They were not out to make money as a business concern, but even if they were it would be had policy to give up. it would be better to face the chance of a few trying years and be in a position to take advantage of the good times which would follow the declaration of peace. The Society would win through if they would only not get dis- pirited. The report and balance .lieet having been adopted, Mr. W. H. Payton proposed an altera- tion of the rule relating to the formation of the committee, which will now be ,composed of eleven members, including three ladies. The inclusion of ladies in the committee would be an innovation, he said, but, in view of the tre- mendous amount of work which the ladies had clone in war time, he thought it was abundantly justified. Furthermore, he believed their advice would be of great assistance to the com- mittee. (Hear, hear.) Mr. G. H. Smith seconded, and remarked that even in normal times the ladies constituted the bulk of the membership of the Society, but hitherto, although they had worked as actively, they had had no part in . the managenfent: Their claims to representation were intensified at the present time, when alms* the whole of the vocal membership consisted of ladies. The nuniber was limited to thr4 ladies so that the men could protect themselves and avoid their conferences being turned into sewing meetings. (Laughter.) The following male members were then elected: Messrs. J. Sephton, 4. W., Bibby, A. Fieldhouse, J. E. Hannan. -C4'. H. 'Smith, W. Hipkiss, J. B. Poole and J. Newall. Tie nominations of the ladies were postponed, the idea being that they should take time to con- sider the names. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart.. was re-elected president, Mr. Lionel Fairclough hon. secretary, Mr. J. P. Reynolds hon. treasurer, Mr. J. Scot- son hon. registrar, Mr. "W Brown hon. auditor, and Mr. Herbert Geary was elected librarian.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Mrs. Garrett-Anderson's Pioneer MAicz.,l Work.

It is hoped that the health of- Mrs. Garrett- Anderson will permit of her being present when the Queen opens the London School of Medicine for Women early in October. just fifty years ago this autumn, aTter overcoming what seemed insuperable difficiilties, in winning her medical degree, Mrs. Anderson set up a modest dispensary fw the treatment of diseases of members of her own sex. The event was scarcely noted by the Press or public at the time, but this modest beginning developed in course of time into the New Hospital f3r Women. In 1889 the present tine building in Euston-road, London, was completed and opened by King Edward, then Prince of Wales. This hospital has sent forth such students as Dr. Selina Fox, the present governor 'of the Female Convict Prison at Aylesbury, and other lady doctors who are rendering valuable service to the nation as inspectors of reformatory and in- dustrial schools, among whem may be named Dr. H.t E. Harrison and Miss Ada Whitlock, M.B. The magnificent building now being raised in India's new capital. Delhi, as a Women's Medical Collegr, is modelled on the plan of the London School. There are at pre- sent about eleven hundred women on the medi- cal register.

Forestry for Womon. Forestry offers a profitable and interesting career to women. Hitherto this has been re- garded as men's work, but as nearly all the male students have been- taken for the Army, there is a lack of trained experts. Continental countries have for a long time employed women\ as workers in nurseries for trees, but the idea is a new one in Britain. University College,' North Wales, has an experimental farm for seed beds and nurseries, and this is under the superintendence of a lady, the first to gain the B.Sc. in forestry. The whole course is now thrown open to women, and it is hoped that a greater number will avail 'themselves this winter of the opportunity to qualify as graduates in this department of agriculture. The women employed on the University College farm live in a hostel and 'share the honsekeep- ing. The working hours are from seven in the morning until five, with an hour and a half for meals. The work requires a cerfain amount of physical strength, but it is healthy and very fascinating. Simple Names for High-born Babies. ...

There are fashions in feminine names, as in dresses, and every generation Or two definite changes set in, so that it orten possible to tell from her name, in which decade of the 19th century a woman was born. Bible names were very popular in the early Victorian days, but these gave way to the more sentimental Evan- geline, Marion, Agnes, &c. and the names chosen for the daughters of Queen Victoria— nearly all of which were foreign. Tennyson's liroines were very popular for a long time, and the Crimean War was responsible for a great number of Almas, and other Russian names, some rather barbaric in sound. Jewel names had a vogue nok So very long ago, favourites being Pearl, Ruby and Beryl. The Diamond Jubilee provided a name for many girls born in the year of its' celebration, and then change being , a necessity, flower names came in and Violet-3 Roses, Lilies, Pansies and Mays bloomed. Rose- mary, which in French is so musical, has recently returned to favour, with Ruth, Joan, Peggy, Betty and Anne, in fact any simple old- fashioned name is chosen just now by parents moving in the highest social circles for their baby daughters. The coat and,skirt costume found a formid- able rival in tlfe'coat-frock of last spring, this winter it will have two others to dispute its sway, in the overcoat and cape. The long coat in fur, or in blanket cloth, has been worn for some years as wraps, but this autumn it will be seen in a variety of form's, and in all lengths,

COMFORTS FOR SOLDIERS.

Mr DEAR. CHILDREN,

The soldiers would be very well looked after if you had your way. Doubtless you will help your parents to supply as many comforts as 'is possible. This, I think, will be the fast winter for them in the trenches,i and we must all do the best we can to make it the most successful from our point of view. As you know, the better the men are looked after, the better they will be able to fight our battles. So let us keep on sewing 4nd knitting while we keep the home fires burning! The prize this week goes to 524 Niece Eva M. Morraii,

47, Price-street, who joined the Corner a long

_ while ago, when it was only half as large as it is now. This is the third time she has won a prize, so that she will be a' strong candidate for promotion when Uncle Ned returns from the wars and begins his scheme for awarding honours to his successful nephews and nieces. This week we will have for competition an essay entitled My Opinion of the Victory of the Somme. I know all the facts: I want to know what you think about them.

Your loving - UNtLE JACK.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WINNING ESSAY.

Niece .Eva M. Morrell, 47, Price-street.— "Although we hold the upper hand now on all fronts and the final victory is fast approaching, there is every likelihood of a severe winter campaign. For this we must be prepared. Our valiant soldiers must be supplied with warm clothing and other comforts. Mittens, scarves, woollen helmets and warm socks are being knitted by the girls and women who, knowing they cannot fight, do their duty in this way. I am glad to say I' am doing my share. Then the soldiers need those luxuries, yet neces- sities, such as cigarettes, Oxo and those( ventions of the war—if I may call them so— compressed tea, coffee, cocoa, milk tablets, also patent pocket stoves. Our armies are fighting for us; let us do something for them."

===== 0002601_19160930_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGHLY COMMENDED.

N i ec.e Doris Whittaker, 62, Baxter-street (Evelyn-street School).—" I am glad to say that all the soldiers and sailors are getting comforts by children knitting socks and het-, mets. It is coming\winter and the soldiers will want strong boots and socks because of tramp- ing in the snow and walking in .the trenches when it has been raining hard. If I had my way I would send all the soldiers a pair of socks. Many comforts are needed by those who are in hospital, sick or wounded." . Niece Emily Marshall, 183, Winwick-road (Silver-street School).—"We are now in the midst of a great war and We will have to fight our way through it. Soldiers are fighting for us, and now as the winter comes on we ought to give a bit of money or buy wool and knit for them. When we send them things wp are paid back; it is a good job gor us that we were in England instead of Belgiu`zn. They fight and give their lives for us, so we should pay, them back in other ways. Some comforts which they enjoy are cigarettes, tobacco, mittens, choco- late, scarves and gloves. We should all knit or send them parcels." Nephew Frank Edwards, 8, Monks-street (Heathside School), says that a small spirit lamp easily taken to pieces so as to be carried in a

There are many of these, and thq following have proved a success in our home and are inexpensive. Newspapers, tar. paper, and kero- sene are good. If these are properly used, no trace of the evil works of the moths will b 3 found. Boxes are best to store the things away in. Spray the corners and bottom with kero- sene, then fill over the bottom and sides with plenty of old newspapers- the garments and other articles. Cover with a thick layer of news- papers, tucking down the corners and' side. Spray the cover of the box with kerosene. Moths will not lay eggs in a protected place like this. In the fall, if the materials are hung out in the open-air and sunshine, all odour will evaporate. I know of a friend who •hangs suits, coats, furs, &c., in the open attic in bags, with just a small piece of cotton odourised with kerosene in •one of the pockets, and she says nothing is ever touched by moths.—" Indiana Farmer."

soldier's pocket is a great 'boon to men at the Front.

Nephew George Emery, 69, Lythgoes-lane.— " Winter is drawing near, and every possible effort is needed to provide comforts for our Tommies at the Front. The chief •of these comforts is the sheep's fur which is made into a kind of jacket; the next are the mits and socks which many people will be busy making during the winter. Another is the woollen helmets, which help to keep the frost from their faces. Many igarmers will be very busy about this time of the year selling the furs to the Army." Niece Amy Campbell, 56, Lancaster-street (Evelyn-street School).—" Winter, as you say, is approaching, and the comforts for our soldiers at the Front must be our first thought. What I consider to, be most essential are good warm shirts and socks. Then come mufflers, mittens, and helmets. After these come the neat little packages of Oxo cubes, tea and sugar tablets, tinned milk, with the various other commodities with which 'Tommy' is able to make himself a good hot drink with the addition of a little boiling water. Last, but not least, come the ever-welcome soap, cigarettes and matches."

Niece Jane Gough, 5, Phillip-street.—" Now that the cold weather has come our soldiers and sailors need comforts, ,and some need comforting. One way of comforting lonely soldiers that have no friends to write to them is to write kind, friendly letters to them. We can find lonely soldiers by writing to our own friends, asking them to send up the name and address of any soldier whom they know gets no letters. Another way is to knit socks and mittens and to send parcels to them, and make them warm and happy. While the war is on we must be more self-denying and do without the luxuries which we had before the war. They must be given to the soldiers and sailors who are making such sacrifices for us and our country."

Niece Lily Healey, 85, Hoyle-street.--" I think that helnbets, socks, scarves and mitts are most useful to our soldiers while they are at the Front; especially mitts, which are much more useful to them than gloves, because they need not take them off when firing. It is very hard to say which they need most as it is so very cold and wet out in France that they need all the comforts they can get to keep them warm."

Niece Elsie Johnson, 323, Lovely-lane (Viry- cliffe).—" Why, the greatest comfort for our soldiers would be for them to win the war! Other comforts, which differ much from these, are those made at home by their loved ones and by different societies at home and abroad. Cuffs and woollen helmets are worn by our soldiers in winter to protect them from being frost-bitten."

Niece Maud Alice Lunny, 108 a, Bridge-street .Arpley-street School).—" Soldiers at the Front need socks very much to keep their feet in good condition for marching; also shirts, mit- tens and warm things for the winter. Now the weather is coming colder, warm clothes are mostly needed. Soldiers in the trenches need more comfortable things because they are ex- posed to all kinds of weather, having to stay there nearly all 06 time. The ladies are well thanked for their presents, and many a nice letter from the soldiers has been sent."

FARM AND GARDEN..

COUNTY FARMERS' HELP FOR ALLIED FARMERS.

Lancashire and Cheshire farmers, who have raised over £13,000 for the Agricultural Relief of Allies Fund, have brought the fund to ,its first £lOO,OOO as the result of the gift sale at Chester opened by Katharine Duchess of West- minster. The £3,000 which was realised by the sale lifts the fund to £100,674, but the com- mittee aim. at raising much more in order to make an adequate response to the cry for help which will be made from the agricultural dis- tricts of Serbia, Belgium and Poland when the enemy is driven back. Of the amount so far raised over £60,000 has been secured by means of farmers' gift sales, and four such sales alone held in Cheshire—at Chester, Knutsford, Altrincham,‘ and Macclesfield—have produced £9,422 for this fund.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
CABBAGES FOR SPRING.

Now is the time to plant out the main lot of cabbages for spring use. The young, plants from seed sown in July will now be in fine condition for setting out, more especially after the copious rains we have had. A few rows of the strongest plants should be set out on a warm, sheltered border to come in in March. For the main crop to come in in May there is no need to dig the ground. That from which the onion crop has been cleared answers well for the spring cabbages. Such ground having been well manured and deeply dug for the onions all that is necessary is to hoe it over and clear off the weeds. The firmness of the onion bed will check gross growth, which, should the winter be severe, is sure to suffer. If it is thought that the cabbages will benefit by feeding them in spring, then some manure placed between the lines will do good. If rotten manure, as is likely to be the case in these days of motors, is unobtainable, then recourse must be had to some artificial manure, such as sulphate of ammonia or nitrate of soda. The newer intro- ductions in cabbages, such as Harbinger, April, and Flower of Spring, are so dwarf and compact that they may be planted very closely, the plants as the hearts become fit for use being pulled up bodily, thus making room for those that are left to increase in size.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
MILK AS IT CAME FROM THE COW.

The country papers frequently contain re- ports of Milk prosecutions which fail owing to the defendant pleading the High Court ruling in Hunt v. Richardson. A majority of judges held that milk, in order to be genuine milk, need not be up to a standard if it could be proved to have been sold just as it came from the now. Attempts have been made, and will be made, to override, or to evade, the ruling in Hunt v. Richardson. In a recent case the prosecution contended that "the supply to such an institution as a hospital involved a higher obligation than the mere sale to a per- son in the street." The defendant, a reputable farmer, had been supplying milk to a muni- cipal hospital for a number of years on a written contract that the milk should contain not less than 3.5 per cent. of fat, that being 1.5 per cent. above the Board of Agriculture I standard. After' the expiration of the last written contract he was allowed to continue to supply milk to the hospital. That,, it was con tended by the prosecution, assumed that the future supply would be in the terms of the written. contract.- No proof was offered that the milk had been tampered with in any way, or that it was otherwise than as it came from the cow. The suggestion that the defendant was bound by the terms of a contract that had in fact ceased to exist, does not appear to have been taken seriously, and in the result the case was dismissed. Presumably, though the reports do not say so explicitly, the ruling in Hunt v. Richardson prevailed with the Bench.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19160930_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

RAMBLER ROSES.

In most gardens nowadays the rambler roses find a place, and in order that plenty of flowers may be forthcoming next year attention must now be given. to the thinning-out of the old and exhausted growths. As is well known, the rambler roses flower best on the., ripened woon made the previous year, so in order to admit light and air to ripen up the shoots, all the rods that have bloomed should be removed as soon. as the flowering is over. In the case of plants that are only two or three years old some of the old canes may be left if not too exhausted.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 30, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
THE " MAJESTY " OF THE LAW-AT WARRINGTON.

We are supposed to be living in Spartan- like days, in which the mamby-pamby cus- toms of a soft-hearted, and perhaps softer- headed, time have, for a season, been dis- carded. Men are, giving their lives for their country as light-heartedly as they formerly ran the milder risks Of the field of sport; women are suffering in silence for the loss of that which can never be replaced. We are getting. "harder," approach more nearly the actualities of life, and are banishing a flabby sentimentalism that had outlived its possi- bilities for good. We should expect to see our magistrates prepared to fall into line and be prepared, in the circumstances, to mete out justice under the guidance of reason rather than under an impulse .to lean to the side of "mercy " where mercy is entirely mis- placed. We were always under the impres- sion that mercy and kindly consideration were misplaced, and even misnamed, when they were invoked to save the skin of a ruffian who has assaulted and perhaps injured for life a policeman whose only offence has been that he has been doing his duty by the State by endeavouring to see that its laws were not broken. Mercy to such miscreants means the withdrawal of mercy from the representatives of law and order. When the innocent suffer and the wrongdoer is treated leniently mercy is a misfit, and becomes neither him that gii,e3 nor him that takts. The offender should be prepared to take his punishment and our magistrates should be prepared to administer it. If this is so in normal times the argument applies with manifold strength now. The Warrington Police Force has been reduced, in round figures, from 90 members to 45, the balance being away fighting in the cause of national safety and international right. To make up the deficiency about 150 volunteers have come forward to help to police the town. It is-essential that this remnant of the regular force and their voluntary helpers should have ithe power of the State behind them, wholly and unequivocally, or a most undesirable state of affairs at once prevails. Fortunately the town is, on the whole, peaceably inclined; it is, in fact, behaving its'elf most creditably. But there are some few, in some cases strangers, who are indisposed to comply with the rules that go to make up respectable and law-abiding citizenship. One of these excep- tions came to the surface on Saturday night. A half-drunken man was detected by Special Inspector MASON in Barbauld-street shouting to women and behaving in an indecent man- ner. The police have special instructions to cleanse the streets of this villainous Class of ' offender, and Inspector MASON 'proceeded to arrest his man. He received a right-hander from the shoulder' on the side of the jaw and a terrific up-and-down struggle ensued, in which another and similar blow was delivered.'

===== 0002601_19160930_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 30, 1916.

With the assistance of three or four other constables the prisoner was conveyed to the Police Station. One would have thought that here was a case in which an example could have been made; but on Monday what appears to have been an unseemly wrangle took place among the eight magistrates who heard the case. Presumably some wanted to commit to gaol, others to inflict a small fine. They were evenly divided, and the MAYOR, very wisely refusing to let u ruffian escape, ad- journed the case so that it might be heard by a fresh Bench on Tuesday. The prisoner, on being re-charged on Tuesday, was fined 30s. or a month's hard labour.

As the prisoner was a poor man he went to gaol, which, in the result, was satisfactory. But we hold that these assaults upon the police should, unless there are strong reasons to the contrary, be followed by gaol without the option .of a fine. Had the prisoner in this case been a little better off, he would, without feeling the loss, have 'paid the fine; therefore, be has gone to gaol not becau-se he seriously maltreated a public official; but I.)te_.ause be was Door, which is absurd. We hope , the magistrates will, in future, see the wisdom, to mention the dignity, of pro- viding that those who are maintaining the law are duly respected when • they bring cases before them. • Otherwise, magistrates will merely be parties to bringing the law into contempt. Tears for prisoners are all very well, hut practical sympathy for those who are endeavouring to prevent and to punish wrong- doing is much better.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
N12.31-IT3OM MEN'S STARTLING

ULTIMATUM.

The town has been threatened this week-end with a strike that would have endangered the public health and rendered us liable to the worst effects of accumulated refuse. The Sanitary Works Committee that met on Tues- day were confronted by a " request " from the ash men and nightsoil men and drivers for an increase of no less than 10s. a week in their wages. It was intimated that the men had' given notice last Saturday to leave their jobs if the request were not complied with by to-day. So that unless the committee were prepared to capitulate we were to have a strike, and the filth of the borough was to be left in the thousands of unsavoury receptacles that have been crowded in all our back premises. The system is wrong and must be put right; but that is another, question. What we are con- cerned about is that any body of working men should propose to injure a community by making bad worse if they .cannot have their exorbitant demands conceded without ques- tion or delay. We are glad to say that wiser counsels have prevailed, and that the notices have been withdrawn, the men, having agreed to the committee's suggestion that the " dis- pute" should be submitted to the arbitration of the Board of Trade. It may be pointed out that at present the drivers receive a wage of 31s. a week and a bonus of 25., and the car- riers a wage of 28s. and 3s; bonus, and the General Purposes Committee have just de- cided to give an additional war bonus of 2s. to all married male employes, which will take over £3,000 a year to pay, or an additional 3d. in the £ on the rates. Thus it will be seen that the Corporation has acted not un- generously by its employes. We do not think that the men are to be blamed for try- ing to do the best they can, for themselves in these days of expensive living. It is the peremptory method and reckless manner in which they have proceeded to which we object and which We feel sure the whole town will resent.
