GOSSIP.

" Maggie " is still unresponsive.

Captain Whittle being a prisoner of war at Offizier Kreigsgefangenenlager, Gutersloh, the Town Clerk has permanently solved intricacies of the address and the laboriousness of writing it by having envelopes printed !- I have specially entrusted the setting of it to a Welsh Linotype operator.

Since Thursday most of the local works have been closed and the workmen have been at rest. I, too, am having a "breather." No smoke is coming from chimney steaks.

The holiday is generally confined' to the works' employes, the staffs labouring on. :-But was it not the holiday spirit which induced two of the lady typists at Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., to change their minds on Thursday as they were on their way to a harvest festival and go to see " Moths " at the Royal Court Theatre? One of them was observed sitting with a. Prayer Book in her hand. Her companion held the programme.

Gentlemen having always had the monopoly of the management of the Musical Society, three, ladies in a committee of eleven, members are now to be admitted to serve and, no doubt, they will be extremely useful.

But will they be shy? At the annual meeting on Tuesday evening, a seconder to a proposition was required. The Chairman looked round appealingly, and one of the ladies modestly gave her acquiescence with a nod and a smile, although the occasion merited a speech. Per- haps she could have sung it better.

It appears that some of the lady members have failed to settle up for concert tickets rt.- ceived last year, and one or tivo, even for the year before. Sheer ,forgetfulness, no doubt. Also the artistic temperament!

It took two sittings of the magistrates this week to come to a decision to punish, in the mildest possible way, a prisoner* who had molested two women, acted indecently, and savagely assaulted a special police inspector.

The Chief Constable made a " special " appeal. With a stinging kind of irony which the second Bench did not apparently notice, h.) asked for the protection of a body of men who had come forward to make good the wastages of war hi the police force. "If it had been one of my own regular force," he said, "I would not have minded so much."

Of course, he did not mean that. He meant that past experience of the attitude of some magistrates had taught him to know that it is little use asking the Bench for their protection. So he, as a matter of rhetorical strategy, threw his "own men if over and bent his energies upon appealing for those who are giving their services to the town. _.

The appeal was but partially successful. To steal " property " of any kind is the nearest way to gaol known to the law. A policeman's jaw does not counts or counts for little.

I see that only ; some of the gas lamps are to have painted 'white bands. So it will be your own fault if you select the posts without bands to run against.

Special Constables. wearing badge and armlet have been given the freedom'of the cars—when on duty.

"There was an attendance of 10,000 on the Warrington ground, where Runcorn were badly beaten," says the "Daily News."

I am a firm believer in the veracity of the newspaper in question, but on the present occasion it would appear that Runcorn were not beaten half so badly by Warrington as was Truth by the "Daily News." The " gate " at Wilderspool was 2,000, which, .for war time, was remarkably good.

His further advance- ment to commissioned rank will be heard of with much lEatisfaction, testifying, as it does, to his many excellent qualities. ===== 0002601_19160930_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
CAPTAIN ARTHUR LEE PICTON.

Majesty's Destroyer Broke went into action on the occasion of that glorious victory over the German Fleet, this gallant officer was saluted as Engineer Lieutenant Commander Picton When :H.M.S. Broke—the last destroyer lA, reach port after the battle—reached the appointed haven three days after the conflict terribly k&cked about and having a sad list of casualties to report—his name had been noted for special mention. When the expected Admiralty announcement was published it was found that, recommended for good service in action, Engineer Lieutenant Commander Picton had been promoted Engineer Commander, with the title of Captain. The promotion was exceed- ingly popular and well earned, and we under- stand that it was one of two cases in which officers of identical rank received the same recognition. • Referring to the vessel, the Admiralty report stated: "H.M.S. Broke was severely damaged and casualties were very great, but the morale appears to have been unshaken and the ship was successfully steamed back to port." Engineer Commander Picton is 38 years of age, and the second son of Mr. and Mrs. J. Picton, " Westleigh," Lower Walton. He was born in Warrington and was educated in the Grammar School. Choosing naval engineering as his future profession, he entered Keyham -College, Devonport, at the age of fourteen, as an engineer student. When war broke out he was at Wei-hai-wei, on the China station, and was called to more active and dangerous waters ! near home, being appointed to H.M.S. Broke. ' He was granted three weeks' leave of absence "‘after the Jutland Battle, and is now at sea again ! with the fleet. Commander Picton 's , elder brother is Major, J. T. Picton, A.S.C., who is in France at the present time. The Commander I married Miss Grime, of Helsby.

Already the holder of the Military Cross, Regimental-Sergeant-Major Talbot ' V. W. Roberts, of the South Lancs. Regiment, has been awarded the Russian decoration, the Medal if St. George (Ist Class). His brother, Mr. Aubrey Roberts; resides at 10, Amelia-street. Sergt,-Major Roberts recently arrived in War- rington for the purpose of spending a short leave with his relatives, but his nealth having broken, he is now a patient in the Lord Derby War Hospital. Sergeant-Major Roberts, who is ,a son of the late Quartermaster-Sergeant J. W. Roberts, of the South Lancs. Regiment, who was for some years stationed at Orford Barracks, has been at the Front almost from the beginning. He was in the retreat from Mons, and for fine work during these trying times he was mentioned in Viscount French's first dispatch. He was awarded the Military Cross, and received it at the hands of the King at Buckingham Palace in May. When quite young, Sergeant-Major Roberts joined the South Lancs. Regiment as a drummer, after having served as a Sergeant- Major in the St. Elphin's Company •9f the Church Lads' Brigade:

===== 0002601_19160930_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
RECORD MAYORALTY.

Aid. Peter Peacock Honoured

===== 0002601_19160930_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
Four Times Running.

A POPULAR CHOICE.

The unanimity of the General Purposes Com- mittee, which consists of the whole of the mem- bers of the Town Council, in inviting Alderman Peter Peacock to become Mayor for a fourth year, is a happy reflection of Public feeling, and we have great pleasure in recording the fact and extending to him our congratulations. By his acceptance a record has been achieved, for, although it is possibre to recollect gentle- men having been the chief magistrate of the borough for four years, Alderman Peacock is the first one to hold it successively.

When the General Purposes Committee met on Mond/ty afternoon, the invitation was soon re- solved upon. Alderman Bennett presided in the unavoidable absence of - the Deputy-Mayor (Councillor Joseph). The proceedings were private, but, like Lobbyists and the Govern- ment, we have been able to gather some inter- esting facts. The proposition that Alderman Peacock should again be invited to fill the- Mayoral chair was proposed by Cauncillor Broadhurst and seconded by Alderman Tinnion—two gentle- men of: different political persuasions—and little was needed to be said in order that the motion should receive unaninpus support. This having been signified, the chairman, proposer and seconder waited upon Alderman Peacock, and asked him whether he would. fall in with their wishes. He agreed, and then entered the Coun- cil Chamber and expressed his thanks for the confidence they had shown in him. In again accepting the position he said he recognised the great honour which the Council had done him in selecting him for four years in succession. He did not under-estimate the heavy responsi- bilities' of the office, especially in such trying days as those through which we Were now pass- ing, but he could assure them that it would be his endeavour, as in the past, to serve all inter- ests and parties impartially; and to devote as much time as possible to the duties. Alderman Peacock was- present at a sale, of work at Brick-street Mission on Thursday, and, in reply to congratulations, made some inter- esting observations recorded in our report of the sale.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
HARMONY IN THE

CHURCHES.

Free Churches' Goodwill

Towards The Anglican Mission.

The Rural Deanery of Winwick are taking; their share in the National _Mission of Repent- ance and Hope next week, and as a token (if goodwill the Free Church Council have addressed the following letter to the Rural Dean (the Rev. Canon Willis) : • Bewsey Manse, Warrington, 27th September, 1916.

Dear Canon Willis, As your Mission of Repentance and Hope begins next week, we should like to assure you and your colleagues and fellow Churchmen in Warrington of the hearty sympathy of the Free Churches. There is • one Lord and one Spirit and one God and Father of all, and we feel that, at this time especially, we should embhasise the great truths that are common to us and think less of the fewer and smaller things in which we differ. We agree with you that everything in our political life and our social and industrial order must be brought into harmony with the mind of Christ, and that moral reformation must spring from spiritual revival.

We are requesting that prayer be offered in all our churches on Sunday next for the suc- cess of your effort, and that the grace of the Holy Spirit may be richly poured out upon you all, and through you upon our beloved land.

Conscious that no Church liveth to itself and confident that your intercession and work will not be in vain. We beg to remain, Yours faithfully. W. T. DERRY. President, Free Church Council, HERBERT OGDEN, Secretary.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
Canon Willis Reciprocates.

To this Canon Willis has replied :--" Dear Mr. Derry and Mr. Ogden,—Please accept and con- vey to your council my very best thanks for your most kind and sympathetic letter. lam sure it. will give great pleasure to our clergy and workers, to whom I will read , it at our next meeting. I cannot tell you how gladly I personally value and appreciate the friendly recognition I receive from all your ministers, and I am sure that all the clergy and laity heartily thank God for your sympathy and prayers.

"With kindest regards, "I am, . "Very truly yours, " FREDERIC W. WILLIS."

In, conversation with our representative on Thursday evening, Canon Willis frankly ex- pressed his pleasure at having received the letter from the Free Church Council, and agreed that it was a new departure significant of much good. "We are on very cordial relations here in Warrington," he added. "Some time. ago I wrote to the Nonconformist ministers and the Roman Catholic clergy to say that we in- tended taking part in the National Mission dur- ing the last week in. October, and I asked 'them if they could see their way to simultaneous action.. I did not ask them to co-operate, be- cause I felt that there were difficulties in the way. I got some very nice replies, but the Roman Catholic clergy mentioned—that they were unable to fall in with the suggestion be- cause they always held their missions in Lent, and the Nonconformist minister./ replied that, at any rate, we should have their sympathy and prayers. That, I can assure, is a great deal, and we esteem their action highly."

Thanks are now being given for the harvest; thanks also are given for the seed of good- will which has just been sown.

"Great Confessions of Faith."

On Sunday mornings during October the Rev. Irvine Lister will deliver, at Cairo-street Chapel, a series of addresses on "Great Confes- sions of Faith." To-morrow's subject will be "The Confessions of St. Augustine."

A Corporal-Chairman.

There was a good meeting on Sunday at the WYcliffe P.S.A., presided over by Corporal W. Carter, who was over on furlough. He gave the meeting a short history of his experiences "Somewhere in France." He has been in many ,:ngagements. He is conscious cif the great in- terest the P.S.A. always takes in its soldier- members. No one, he said, could say When the war would be over, but certainly the Germans were in the grip of the Allies. Rev. T. W. Bond, of St. Helens, gave the nddress, and followed up the remarks of the Chairman by saying it was the duty of those at home to do their bit by making any sacri- fice: they must work well, live well, and save well, and so help the country and our brave defenders to bring the war to an end. The Secretary (Mr. B. 'Farrar), welcomed the Chair- man, Sergeant A. Spencer,- who had been wounded and Was on furlough, and Private Buckley, on leave. A hearty greeting was given to Mr. Robertson, of the Soho Hill Men's Brotherhood, who was present from Birming- ham. Mr. Yearsley And Mr. Healey also took part in the meeting. The Secretary said he hoped to have the prize books ready next Sun- day.

N.S.P.C.C. and Child-Neglect. e The National Society for the Prevention °L Cruelty to Children investigated 3,617 COD plaints of neglect and cruelty in England, Wales and Ireland during the month of August. Of the 3,531 completed cases, 3,401 were found true, affecting the welfare of 10,377 children and involving 4,186 offenders. Warnings were issued in 3,076 cases; 145 were prosecuted (re" suiting in 144 convictions); and 180 were dealt with by transfer or in other ways. From its foundation, in IsB4, the society has dealt with 924,030 complaints, involving 2,591,442 children. In the Warrington Branch during the same month 14 cases were dealt with, affecting 36 children.

Emergency Training for Adults at I'Vpod's College. Splendid results have been achieved at the combined day and evening courses which have been in operation since January at this popular business-training centre. The course is of f" weeks' 'duration, and the fee is three guineas. Quite a large number of ladies have been sae' cessful in acquiring .a sensible and practical working knowledge of routine clerical duties, which has enabled them to accept well-remuner- ated positions as general or departmental clerks in many of the largest offices in the district. -A fair general education is an essential for pros" pective members, the training is comparativell easy of acquirement, and really good prospect! are open to ladies between 18 and 25 years 01 age. For further information address: The Secretary, Wood's College, 15, Bridge-street, Warrington.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 30, 1916. PRIVATE DUKES.

Trade Unions To Fill His Place On Pensions Committee.

STILL ANOTHER MEETING.

The nominations of Private Charles Dukes (at the time he being a civilian) and Mr. A. E. Seabury as Labour representatives on the War Pensions Committee not having been accepted by the Town Council owing to their anti-war policy, the matter was referred to the Statutory Committee in London, and, as the outcome a it, a meeting of trade unions representatives is to be held at the Town, Hall on the 9th October.

The letter which the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) has sent to the various unions reads as follows: "The Statutory Committee have up- held the objection of the Corporation to the nomination of Mr. Charles Dukes as a member of the local committee. With- regard to Mr. Seabury, the objection is,. disallowed; conse- quently there is now a vacancy, and I am re- quested by the Statutory Committee to com- municate the decision to- you. It will itow be necessary to meet for the selection of- another representative in place of Mr. Dukes,. '4nd I shall be glad if two representatives of your union will attend a meeting to be held at the Town Hall on the 9th of October at 8 p.m., in order to make the nomination, and I shall be pleased to receive the names of the delegates beforehand."

A letter whieh somewhat ampli4.es the intro- ductory paragraph of that issued by the Mayor is now appended. It is written by the secre- tary of the Fabian Research' Department and addiessed to the secretary. of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, Mr. A. E. Seabury holding, that position during, the absence of Private Dukes. .

"Mrs. Sidney Webb, who is one of the repre- sentatives of Labour on the Statutory Com- mittee administering the Naval' and Military Pensions Act, has asked me to write to you on the question of the objection brought against Mr. Dukes •and-Mr. Seabury as 'representatives of the Trades Council on the Warrington local committee. It was reported that Mr. Dukes is at present under the military Service Act and thereby unable to serve on the committee, and to this objection Mrs. Webb and Mr. Gosling could bring-no opposition. In the case of your other representative, they refused'to•allow that •any valid objection had been brought; and the Statutory Committee has accordinglY refiised to sustain the objection of the Warrington Town Council to' your other- representative. Mrs. Webb and Mr. Gosling-ask me to' say that they hope the Trades Council 'will find this satigfae- tory ; they also suggest that it would be well to make no further agitation on the question of Mr. Dukes' position."•

VOLUNTEERS AND HOME DEFENCE.

Lord French's Forecast.

Lord French ingpected Volunteer battalions at Liverpool on Wednesday, a detachment of the Warrington Company of the ,-7th Lancashire Battalion being present. - - _

Addressing the commandants and officers, he said the turniaut reflected great credit on every- body. The men appeared to have the makings of a very efficient body. He wanted the com- mandants and officers to impress on the men that we were living in very serious times, when the services of every man were required, and he hoped they would do all they could to dispel the ridiculous idea that had got abroad that the Volunteers were not wanted, and that the authorities did not pay much importance to them. That was absolutely false and erroneous, and he could assure them that their services were appreciated to the utmost by the War Office.

He was sure they would do their utmost to make the Volunteers, what they promised to be, a really efficient and fighting body of men. If the war went on the time might come when they would look to the Volunteers for the defence of this country. That was the test for which they must prepare themselves. The Warrington Company (under the com- mand of Lieut. 3¢athias) was strongly repre- sented. The men joined their battalion at head- quarters (Toxteth Hall), and, headed by their fine bugle b§tnd, marched to the Pieqead, where they took up their position for the inspection. The smart serge uniforms of the Warringtonians were in striking contrast to the khaki and sombre " green-grey " drill of the Liverpools, and were favourably commented upon. The detachment was afterwards entertained to tea at headquarters, and the Commanding Officer (Major Fraser) expressed his satisfaction at the appearance and general bearing of the men.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
SUMMER TIME.

A Successful Innovation Which Ends To-day.

The Press Bureau issues the following At 3 a.m. (summer time) in the morning of Sunday, Ist October, Greenwich time will be restored by putting back the clock to 2 a.m. The hour 2-3 a.m. summer time will thus be followed by the hour 2-3 a.m. Greenwich time. All railway clocks and clocks in post offices and Government establishments will be put back one hour, and the Government requests the public to put back the time of all clocks and watches by one hour during the night of Saturday-Sunday, 30th September-lst October. Employers arc particularly recommended to warn all their workers in advance of the change of time. From Ist October onwards Greenwich time will be used for all purposes. By Order of the Home Secretary.

Trains and the Time Table.

The Railway Executive Committee give notice that trains starting on Saturday night, September 30th, or early on Sunday morning, October Ist, which are en route at 3 a.m., when the clocks are put back one hour, will after that time be running one hour earlier than the time shown in the official guides. In addition to those, certain other trains will start and run one hour before the time shown in the official time-tables. Particulars of the trains affected will be an- nounced by the companies concerned.

SHOP HOURS AND THE LIGHTING

ORDER. To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—ln View of the corning Order for the closing of all shops a 7 p.m., would it be pos- sible for our authorities to make an Order that this bo the time for the lowering or extinction of lights in shop and business premises? If such an arrangeusent could be4nade the advan- tages to all are obvious. In the fiTst place it would do away with the vexatious search for the 'correct " Zepp." time, which differs nightly, and sometimes leads to a sharp argument with the police on duty. Further, the shopkeeper would be able to do his business in comfort until the hour of closing, when window lights would be extinguished and there would be. no question as to whether they were sufficiently subdued.—Yours, &e., J. H. HOUGHTON. 28, Mersey-street, Warrington, September 20th, 1916.

KILLED.

SERGEANT A. HELSBY.

Going To Help Wounded Corn-

rade When Fatally Hit.

Mr. and Mrs. Hewitt, 4, ,Wellington-street, have been notified that their son, Sergeant Arthur Helsby, a member of the Canadian Ex- peditionary Force, has been killed in action. A comrade writing to Mr. and Mrs. Hewitt said he was killed while running to help a man who was wounded; and he died as he had lived, a soldier and a man. _ _

Sergeant Helsby, who emigrated to Canada seven'years ago, enlisted in Tune, 1915, and had been at the Front for thirteen months J ~,He was 29 years of age, and worked .as a moulder for the Pear Son and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., before going to Canada. He was educated At the National School, Church-street.

RIFLEMAN T.- RYAN.

Eighteen-Year-Old Youth Killed

===== 0002601_19160930_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
In Action.

Only eighteen years of age, Rifleman Thos. Ryan, of the Rifle Brigade, son of Mr. and Mrs. N. Ryan, 40, Lythgoes-lane, has been killed in action. The news first reached Warrington through the medium Of a letter which a com- rade, J. Foster, wrote to his wife at 72, Dalton Bank, saying that Ryan had' "gone under." Mrs. Ryan immediately ;wrote to the War

Killed.

Office, and received a reply to the effect that her son was killed in action on the 3rd September. Rifleman Ryan wrote his last letter home on August 15th, and said: "I think the good old times are ended, but let us hope for the best. Things are looking up a bit out her." He sent a field card to his mother on September 3rd—the very day he was killed— saying he was quite well. Rifleman Ryan; who was formerly employed by Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd. joined the Army on the ,2nd October, 1914, and had been at the Front twelve months. Ile was educated at St. Benedict's R.C. School, and has two uncles serving at the Front.

Fine Tributes From Officers

And Men.

Mr. and Mrs. T. W. Brazendale, 26, Thelwall- lane, have been informed of the death in Action of their son, Private Herbert Brazendale, of the South Lancs. Regiment.

An officer, writing on September 16th, said: "These are just a few lines written on behalf of myself and the officers of B Company, to express to you our sorrow in the death of your son, and our sympathy with you in your grief. Liked both by officers and men, he was a good soldier and is a great loss to the company. I trust that it will be some consolation to you to know that he died doing his duty." PRIVATE 'HERBERT BRAZENDALE.

Killed.

under the same date :—'

" It is with 'a sad heart

, that I write. No doubt by the time you get this letter you will have heard that your son was killed in action yesterday, the 15th, along With other members of the battalion. We were a little 'way from the front line at the time, and the Germans were shelling rather heavily. One shell burst only a yard or two away and killed your son. I know it will be a great blow to you to lose such a dear lad. I can assure You he was loved by all of us here, and will be greatly missed. .. . It may be some con- .solation to you to know that your son died While doing his duty for his country, and the home he loved dearly. I pray God will be with YOU in the sad loss of one, who along with Others, has been called upon to make the great sacrifice. Please accept the deepest sympathy of all the men, of his idatoon, for we all feel WO have lost a true and dear friend.- Private Brazendale, who, as a boy, attended the Christ Church SChool, Latchford, would lave been twenty-two years of age on the 13th "of next month. He belonged to the South Lancs. Regiment prior to the outbreak of the War, and has been in the Army altogether about three years. He went to the' Front twelve taonths last February. He was formerly enlployed as a moulder by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., and attended Christ Church, Latchford.

PRIVATE P. L. ROWE.

Conflicting News As To His Fate Received By His Mother.

Private Paul L. Rowe, Grenadier Guards, and son of the late Mr. P. J. and Mrs. Rowe, is reported by two persOns to have been killed, and by two more to be wounded. The letters which we reproduce below have been received by his mother, :who resides with her son-in-law, Mr. B. B. Ashbrook, at 129, Lovely-lane. , The first letter, dated September 24th, from a comrade, ran, "Just a few lines concerning your son Paul. We have been chums since we joined the Army, and we agreed to write to each other's parents if anything happened to either of us. I am sorry to say that I now find it my very painful duty to write and: let you know that Paul was killed in the trenches on September 14th. He put his head too high over the trench and 'a sniper shot him through the head He was very well liked by all his platoon. Only a few weeks ago the Platoon Sergeant asked him to take promotion, but he refused." .

The same post brought a letter from the Rev. J. Leo Prescott, which ran, "Your son Paul has asked me to write to you to tell you of his wound. I am afraid it is serious. . . . Don't be surprised if worse news follows. I have given him the last sacrament. We have many bad cases, hence my short rioter" The third letter was received on Wednesday from the Rev. P. Casey, who said, "Your son Paul asks me to let you,know he is wounded and gone to hospital—not seriously I hope. He will soon get all right. He sends his kindest regards." •

A fourth letter vats also received from a clearing station, dated September 24th, "I very

PRIVATE J. STEWART. Killed. -

FURTHER NEWS OF CAPTAIN 0. LYON WHITTLE.

The Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) has received a telegram from the American Express Co. at Rotterdam stating: "Captain Whittle quite well; prisoner at Offizirr Kreigsgefangen- enlager, Giitersloh." . The Flying Corps Aid Committee have in- formed Mr. Whittle that the camp at Giitersloh is one of the best. The prisoners are in a large building (originally a lunatic asylum) and. have .a tennis court and are allowed to walk outside the camp three times a week. They are able to buy a good many things, but the rations provided are not at all good, and it is very necessary to send them regular parcels.

Tennis Club and Wounded Soldiers. Last Saturday was the third occasion on which the 'Stockton Heath Tennis Club entertained wounded soldiers from the local hospitals. Tea was provided, and the members and their guests passed a pleasant evening at tennis and other games.

PIIIVATE JAMES DENTON. Died of Wounds.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
of the South Lancs. Regiment, died of wound's on September 17th. • About a week ago Mrs. Denton received two letters saying her son was wounded. The first, from Second-Lieutenant Turner, ran as follows: "I regret that Private Denton has been wounded, but it is not a serious wound, so there Is no occasion to worry on your part. For my part I shall be sorry to lose him from my platoon, and shall be pleased to see him back again:"

A comrade, Private B. Lynsky, wrote: "Just a few lines to let you know that James has been wounded, but not seriously, so you don't aeed to worry, as I hope he will be in England shortly. He was very cheerful when we left him at the dressing station." Private Denton, w,ho was twenty-three years •)f age, had been in the Army almost two years, and at the Front fifteen months. A native of Stockton Heath, whqe his family had resided until recently, he attended St. Thomas! School. Leaving 'there, he went to work for Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co. Ltd., where he was , employed as wmoulder until joining the Army. Another brother, Corporal J Denton, is serving J lin the Royal Engineers.

WIDOW'S GRATITUDE

===== 0002601_19160930_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
To Four Men Who Assisted

Captain Collingwood.

Four brave men who rendered assistance on the Bth August to the late Captain Coiling- wood, of Newton, just before he died, have been presented with silver matchboxes from his widow, the mementoes being engraved with the date mentioned. Two of the four soldiers are Lance-Corimiml Ashworth, son of Mr. and Mrs. John Ashworth, of 15, Wakefield-street, and Private Charles Cranston Taylor, son of Mr. and Mrs. W. 'Taylor, of 1, Kendrick-street. ,With the iiresents came a letter to each, a typical one being: " Dear . The, enclosed is a small remembrance for you for being one a the four men who carried my husband. Captain Collingwood, when he was so badly wounded, to the dressing station on August Bth. Thank you very much. With best wishes for your safe return. Yours sincerely (signed) M. C. C. Collingwood." It may be added that when the lads left the Captain at the dressing station he remarked to them, "God bless you!"

PRIVATE W. BROADHURST.

Former Crosfield Employe Dies

Of Wounds.

Private William Broadhurst, of the South Lancashire Regiment, son of Mrs. Broadhurst, a widow, 5, Atherton-street, has died of wounds in a French hospital. In 'response to a tele- gram received on Tuesday morning saying he was lying dangerously ill, Mrs. Broadhurst cr6ssed to France to see him. Later, a letter reached Warrington from the assistant matron at the hospital stating that Private Broadhurst was seriously wounded in many plaees,, but was improving slightly. Yesterday morning, how- ever, the sad news, was received from the War Office that Private Broadhurst passed away on September 27th. A message of sympathy from the King and Queen was also received.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMORIAL SERVICE TO THE LATE CORPORAL J. BRIMELOn.

On Sunday night, in the Working Men's Mission, Bank Quay, Coun. G. Dale, in the course of a memorial service to the late Corporal J. Brimelow, said their young ,brother, whose loss they, mourned,' had finished his course at the age of 31, just when life seemed full of promise and hopeful- ' ness. Under conditions that were by no means ideal he had tried to do his duty as a Christian. He lived not for selfish ends, but to a very • large extent to help and 'bless others: He was associated with the Sunday school,, and intihe Christian Endeavour Society the members had looked forward hopefully to the end of ,the war with the expectation of having brother "Joe," with his cheery', smiling face, once more in their midst. 'But away out yonder on the battlefields of France the angels beckoned him and he followed. , And in connection with the League of Help, the lantern lectures and the Order of 'Rechabites he was inspired by the same spirit of self-abnegation. He had fought the good iight and had kept the faith, and in these trying: days of doubt and scepticism they were glad to know that he had kept the faith. They would think of their heroic brother in the coming days as one among the' noble sons of Britain who had fought the good fight and ha'l kept the faith and who, in aoing this. had "Scught to aspire to the highest ideals that the soul of man could know. To the berea4ed parents and each member of the family so sadly bereaved, also to the one who had their brother lived, would have been the partner. in life of his joys and sorrows, they would say, "Sorrow not as those without hope," but took forward to the time of re-union, and above all remember the words of our Lord: "Let not your heart be troubled; ye believe in God, be- lieve also in Me."

Poor Work and Poor Pay. Poor work for poor pay is just what keeps thousands of workers where they are.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
AGE OF mum

Bishop's Striking Harvest Ser- mon' At Parish Church.

BEER OR BREAD?

"I believe that the ages that are coming will look upon our time as the age of waste," re- marked the Lord Bishop of Bu'rnley in a power- ful sermon relivered at a harvest festival ser- vice in the Warrington Pariek Church oni Thursday evening.

That was perhaps because this country had been given an abundance of the gotta things of be added, and we under-valued. and wasted tliem.. It was _notorious that compared with other lands we wasted our coal instead of using it wisely, and it might be that higher prices would lead some to show more thrift in that

ireetion that they had been willing to eserciBe

in gratitude tcl the Almighty Giver. And then there was food. Those who had travelled in foreign lands knew well how through carelea%3ness and often through ignor- ance we wasted and made little of the food— meat, bread, and other articles—which ,we had enjoyed' in such an abundance here, turning often the most wholesome forms of food into something unwholesome and casting aside mueh that was good. How often had lone seen it not only in the homes Of the rich, but the poor. It was as though we had taken the food gifts of God4nd- thrown them back in His face.

Our Lord filled the people with five barley. loaves and two fishes. In the East of England at present there were scores. of miles of barley; and how would it be used? How had we used it generally in times past? A little of it—that which we counted a lower quality—had been used as food for cattle, but the best of it and the most of it had been turned into strong drink. They could, therefore, surely say that the greater part of that gift from God had been useless, as it had been devoted for the ruin of the health•orthe body and of moral life. Were they doing right, especially in these days of comparative scarcity of food, in allowing barley to be used for strong drink, which they could very well do without, and which hAd proved' a snare and a curse to many, including wives of poor soldiers who did not titink they were fighting for homes spoiled by drink? They should learn how to be thrifty with Gbd's gift:s. The Bishop also spoke of the wastage due to infantile mortality and had a word of stricture for pro;sperous, self-centred people, who never did 'anything worth while, and when they went to the grave there was no obvious- vacancy to be filled.

The harvest thanksgiving service, which in- cluded.the singing of the "Hallelujah Chorus,'' was beautifully and impresisvely rendered. Harvest thanksgiving services will be COIT.

;Hine& to,moProw in the Parish Church. The

Rev. C. C. Eleum, Liverpool, is to preach iirthe morning, and the Rev. and Hon:- C. F. Cross, vicar of Stretton, in the evening.

CAI RO-STREET.

Harvest festival thanksgiving services were

Leld at the Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday; the

preacher being the Rev. D. J. Evans, of Chester. An anthem, "0 Lord, how manifold are Thy works" (Barnby), was excellently sung by the choir, Mr. James Muir being at the organ. The vicinity of the pulpit had been nicely decorated by Miss Monks and the Misses Pemberton. Afterwards the flowers and fruit were dis- tributed among the local hospitals.

ST. JAMES'.

Harvest thanksgiving services in connection with St. James' Church were -commenced' on Thursday evening, the preacher being the Rev. F. S. Thatford, vicar of St. Paul's, Maccles- field. ST. ANN'S.

At St. Ann's Parish Church on Sunday har- vest festival services were held. The Vicar (the Rev. James Davies) preached in the morning, and the Rev. Horace Gray (headmaster of the Grammar School) delivered a sermon in the evening. Special music was rendered by the choir.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET WESLEYANS.

The IBold-street -Wesleyan Church harvest thanksgiving services, held on Sunday, were largely attended, and the collections taken vn behalf of the Society Funds were considerably in advance of those of last year'. The preacher at the morning service was the Rev. F. Hudson, who recently began work in the Warrington Circuit. The choir gave, a beautiful rendering of Smart's " Te Deum." The Rev. P. Meson, who has just completed twelve months of his ministry in charge of the Bold-street., Church, was the preacher at the evening service. An anthem, " ,Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem," was sung by the choir, Miss Emily Taylor taking the solo.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. PETER'S.

Harvest thanksgiving services were continued at St. Peter's Church on Sunday, in the morn- ing, afternoon and evening. The preacher morning and evening was the Rev. H. S. Bolton, vicar of Ravenhead, and in the after- noon the Rev. T. M. Armour, curate of St. ElPhin's. Special music was rendered by the choir, Mr. E. A. Padmore being at the organ. At the morning service the anthem, "While the earth remaineth," was rendered, and in

the evening Simper's " Magnificat " and " Nunc Dimittis," 'and an anthem, "Ye shall dwell in.. the land," were sung with taste and expression. Solos in the anthem were taken by Miss Mabel Percival and Mr. 11. Sudlow. There wete good congregations at each service, and collections were taken on behalf of special church expenses.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAPPENHALL INDEPENDENT METOODISTS.

At the Grappenhall independent Methodist Church on Sunday the harvest festival was cele- brated. Mr. Mort, of Lymm, was ,the preacher both morning and evening, and the choir ren- dered various anthems. In the afternoon a cantata was given by the Friars' Green Methodist Choir. The day was a great success in every way, the fine weather adding a finish to the whole. The proceeds amounted to the sum of £6 14s. 10d., being an increase of £2 on the preceding year. On Monday evening a tea was given, after which followed a short concert, there being both songs and recitations, and finally the fruit, vegetables and flowers, &c., which added to the beauty of the church by their decoration on the previous day, were sold by Councillor R. Henshall. The proceeds, combined with the Sabbath's takings, made a total of .210 3s.

SANKEY BRIDGES WESLEYAN CHURCH.

The harvest festival at the above church was held on Sunday. The preacher.in the morning ' was Mr. Lloyd Jones, and in the evening the Rev. F. Hudson, the newly dppointed minister. At the eveninc, service an anthem was well rendered by the choir, under the leadership of Mr. Pennington, and a quaxtet was taken by Miss Bromley, Miss Gough) and Messrs. H. Butler and J. Webb. The organist was Mr. F. Webb. The church had been beautifully decorated with fruit and flowers by lady mem- bers of the congregation. On Monday evening a fruit banquet was held in the schoolroom, at which there was a large attendance. The Rev. I F. Hudson presided, and he gave an interesting laccount of the manner in which a fruit banquet 'was held in the Transvaal, from which colony the reverend gentleman has just returned. The following programme was given :---Duet, Miss Gough and Miss Morris, "Goat bells "; solo, Mr. 11. Kay, "Asleep in the deep "; solo, Miss Morris, "Children's home"; solo, Miss Gough, "Home land." The children, led by the Chairman, also sang the well-known hymn, "Gentle Jesus." At the close of the pro- gramme a hearty vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman, the artistes,. the decorators, and all who had assisted in the work in connection with the festival. Afterwards the fruit and vegetables were sold and the proceeds were forwarded to the funds of the Y.M.C.A. in their work on behalf of the Army.

STAGE AND KINEMA,

Royal Court Theatre. All tkose interested in the higher class of plays regard Mr. Raynor as a benefactor, for he is one' of the very few gentlemen touring high-class repertoire at the present time.

He has had his own companies for eleven years, and if hp found it upfiiii• work ttlitte earlier years he has now Income eminently successful.

Since starting as play producer he has staged! 75 plays—possibly as many, as onyone else id% the profession. This week at the Royal Court .four plays have been presented—'A Bunch of Violets," "The Dancing Girl," "Moths," and "Lady Huntworth's "Experiment," - "The Winch of Violets," to be given to-night, is the first play which Mr. Raynor ever prriduced. aril happy to intimate that another visit will be paid by this company to Warrington irt January next.

The announcement of Thursday matinees, as well' as those on Mondays, has met with a chorus of approval. The theatre was full on Thurs- day, when " Moths " was produced.

Next week a lively revue., will doubtless diair full Minsk to the Royal Court. This is one of the latest and most amusing farcical feasts of fun, and the title "&u Revoir Paris,' iS enough to suggest an evening of undiluted' amusement. In six scenes, a number of unusual attractions are promised, and the merriment will be coutinuous. Forty first-class artistes will occupy the stage.

The Kinemas.

There is now showing at the Star Kinema House, Church-street, a, fine drama, entitled "Hazel Kirke," and Part I. of the great serial, "Peg o' the Ring." For the first half of next week we are to have Hazel Dawn in "My Lady Ineog.," and for the latter half of the week the "star" picture will be "Little Mary Sunshine."

At the Grand Electric Theatre a fine film, entitled "Niobe "' now holds sway. For the first half of next week that fine picture "The Battle of. the Somme" will make its second appearance in Warrington. Special matinees will he held eac-h day at 1 and 3 o'clock and evening performances at 6.30 and 8.30. The eighteenth episode of "The Diamond from the Sky" will also be screened. The great circus serial, "Peg o" the Ring," will commence dur- ing the latter half of the week, 'and .that great picture, "Carmen."

At the Pavilion, Sankey Green, there is now being screened' "'Four Feathers," a soul-stirring photo play. LOCAL WEDDINGS.

LINDLEY-HOGG.

A wedding of a quiet nature took place at St. Paul's Church on Thursday morning, the Rev. E. Downham officiating, between Mr: Frank Lindley., brother of Councillor Lindley, Bewsey-road, and Miss Eliza Hogg, who for- merly resided with Dr. Jago, of Latchford. The bride, who was given away by Dr. Jago, was neatly attired in a navy blue travelling cos- tume. Mr. Harry Sudlow acted as best man. Following .the eeremony the happy couple left for Blackpool. Numerous presents have been received.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
WILLIAMS-ENGLAND.

The wedding was solemnised at the Padgate Wesleyan Church on Saturday morning last of Miss Mabel England, eldest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Ebenezer England, of Walden, Fearnhead, and Private Alfred Edward Wil- liams, son of Mr. and Mrs. Williams, Earle- street, Earlestown, now serving in the R.A..M.C. at Aldershot. The bride, who was given away by her father, looked very charming in a cream costume, with hat to match, and the brides- maid, Miss Bessie England, sister of the bride, was attired in a blue costume and hat to match. Both carried bouquets of pink carna- tions. The duties of best man were ably car- ried out by Sergeant R. R. Rice, of the Train- ing Reserve Battalion, Whitchurch: A recep- tion was held at the home of the bride, after which the happy couple left for Wallasey,. where the honeymoon was spent, the bride- groom having been granted a few days' leave for the occasion, previous to embarkation. The officiating minister was the Rev. A. J. Morton. The wedding, owing to the war, was of a quiet nature and was by special licence.'

Fewer Births and Deaths.

- During last week 30 births took place in the borough and there were 16 deaths, as against 41 and 23 respectively for the corresponding week of last-year. •

Warrington Market Prices. At Wednesday's Warrington Market the price of new wheat was Bs. 3d. per 701 b., and 4s. to 4s. 3d. per 451 b. was realised for new oats. Flour realised 475. 6d. to 525. Gd. per 2401 b., thirds 2s. 4d. to 2s. 7d. per 201 b., bran and other off als is. 7d. to is. 9d. per 201 b., wheat straw 70s. per ton.

Legh Street Primitive Methodist ' Church. Special services will beheld to-morrow in this church. The Rev. T. A. Young, of Widnes, will preach. On Thursday the Rev. H. Davenport, of Oldham, will preach in the afternoon, and in the evening will deliver a lecture, his subject being "C. H. Spurgeon."

Death of Mrs. Dunn, Stockton Heath. Yesterday the funeral of a most respected resident of Stockton Heath took place, when Mrs. Dunn, of 47, Cawdor-street, was interred in Hill Cliffe Graveyard. Mrs. Dunn was the widow of Mr. Dunn, who was formerly a. traveller for Messrs. E. Richardson and Son, of Bridge-street. Mrs. Dunn was the daughter of the late Mr. Howard, of Sankey Bridges, and sister of the late Rev. Joseph Howard. She be- longed to Bold-street Wesleyan Church, and leaves one son, Mr. Arthur Dunn. Her death was due to pneumonia.

PHOSPHATE GIVES WONDERFUL STRENGTH.

Specialist Tells How To Double Strength And Endurance.

A, great authority on the subject of health, strength, and endurance, in explaining the remarkable strength and endurance obtained from the use of the product known among chemists as bitro-phosphate, clainA it is entirely due to its wonderfnl nerce-building properties. He says, and _J must be admitted that his logic is unanswerable, that, inasmuch as the muscles simply transmit power derived from the nervous system, strong nerves and a big reserve of nervous energy are infinitely more .important than big muscles. Bitro-phosphate, not being a drug or a stimulant, but a food which supplies direct to the brain and nerves the required phosphoric elements, was first prescribed by physicians with astonishing suCC'eSS in the treatment of neurasth9nia, insomnia, nervous- ness, debility, and mental depression. It was soon noticed, however, that, in .addition to permanently relieving nervous disorders, it also ,marvellously increased strength and endurance. The amount usually prescribed is one 5-grain tablet with each meal, and, in view of its com- paratively inexpensiveness, those who wish to increase their strength and endurance, as well as those who suffer from nervous disorders, are strongly advised to give it an immediate trial. We understand that pure bitro-phosphate is -obtainable from H. Y. Ashton, 63, Sankey- street, as well as from other high-class chemists everywhere,

SERGEANT DANDY.

another soldier, who was afterwards identified as George Parkin Hall, a labourer, who had enlisted in the Lancashire- Fusiliers, and who was sentenced to three years' penal servitude for the offence.

It will be remembered that Mrs. Dandy and the relatives of the dead sergeant were de- ceived by Hall's remarkable likeness to the sergeant, but their suspicions were aroused later, and the man was arrested.

Sergeant Dandy was the son of Mr. and Mrs. Frederick Dandy, of Newfields, Lymm, and was 39 years of age! He formerly worked for ,the late Mr. F. 0. Mostoia, fustian cutter. The trial of Hall last November created a great sensation. Mr. Justice Low, charging the Grand Jury, said the peculiarity of the case arose from the circumstance that the prisoner, as was usual in charges of that nature, did not use force to accomplish his purpose, but was alleged to have used artifice amounting to fraud. The woman's husband had gone away to the war, and it was said that the prisoner returned from the war and personated the husband. There were some re- markable features in the case, in that the hus- band had been absent a comparatively short time, something under a year, and yet his sister and other relatives seemed to have be- lieved that the prisoner was the husband. Be- yond question he lived and cohabited for some days and nights with the wife.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MEETING.

Prosecuting Counsel explained that the charge was made under the Criminal Law Amendment Act. A man who committed the offence named against a -woman by _impersonat- ing her husband was deemed guilty under the section, the consent of the woinan, being im- material. Mrs. Dandy was the wife of a ser- geant in the Bth Manchesters who went out to Egypt last autumn, and she kept a baker's shop in Clowes-street, West Gorton.. On the morning of July 16th a neighbour named Dutton brought the defendant into the shop. Mrs. Dandy asked who the man was, and Dutton said "Herbert." Mrs. Dandy said "Never." Explaining the defendant's appearance, counsel said that be- fore entering Mrs. Dandy's shop the defendant went into Dutton's shop and said, "How is Mr. Dutton jetting on?" Mr. Dutton in- quired, "Is it Herbert Dandy?" and the de- fendant answered "Yes." The def.endant ex- plained that he had ,got,an hour off from Whit- worth-street Hospital and asked Dutton to tell his wife and children, but after ,t,ome discus- sion he was induced to go himself, Dutton accompanying him. The wife was suspicious and said, "You have altered," and the de- fendant replied that he had lain for 72 hours without help on the battlefield, and that was sufficient to a,lter anyone. Mr,. Dandy began to press him about personal matters, and in answer to questions he said he had lest his memory. The defendant was undotibtedly like Mrs: Dandy's husband. She sent for her lit s- band's sister, and the sister took him to be Sergeant Dandy. The neighbours were simi- larly deceived. The defendant left at one. Next day she took him home, and he remained with her as her husband for some days. Then Mrs. Dandy became suspicious, and asked him why he was dressed as a private. He said he had broken out of Netley Hospital because they were going to put him in a padded room, and he took the first clothes handy. She asked him what had become of the abscess mark on his neck and tattoo marks. He answered that the first had been filled up and the second burned. out. On July 23rd she went to the hospital with him, and from something said by a soldier she occupied a separate room. Afterwards Mrs. Dandy sent for her brother in Yorkshire, and also for a sister, and both thot.tat the defend- ant was her husband. They took him with them to Yorkshire for a few days, and after his return on August 9th Mrs. Dandy would not allow him to stay in the house until he had proved that he was Sergeant Dandy. She went to the station to see him off, and there he was arrested by Detective-Inspector Thomas.

He said he was married to Mrs. Dandy at Latchford, and that a child was born and Christened there. The witness said he was satisfied, after trying to find an abscess mark on prisoner's neck, that Hall was not Dandy, and he said he would lock him up. To this the prisoner remarked, "It is a fine thing for a man who has bled and fought for his country to be locked up." LAYMIVI

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC. Don't fGrget that to-night's the night, and put your clocks back one hour.

Our timepieces have been continually " lying " just lately, but once again they will have the opportunity to tell the truth.

An extra hour's sleep. What a blessing!

For all of us, bar the poor " Specials " who happen to be on duty at that particular time.

They will find themselves "let in" for an extra hour's vigil.

iNever mind, ,they said they could do it. (Without sweating.)

And if the Zepps come they will already be on duty.

And won't knock their shins on the furni- ture getting out in the dark.,

By the way, what do you think about the Zepp scare last Tuesday morning?

The Volunteers and Specials were soon on the scene; in fact every man notified turned out.

The Specials patrolled one half of the village and the Volunteers the other.

This is the first bit of real work they have been called upon to perform, and it is gratify- ing to know that they performed it satisfac- torily. •

For convenience, the Volunteers have changed their headquarters from the Drill- Hall to the Conservative Club.

This only applies in file case of air raids Will putside members please note?

The password down my way at the present is, "Put that light out there."

Our workers, have just had a well-earned vacation. ,

The principal, works in this district closed down on Thursday for the remainder of the week.

Who was the person that wouldn't put hi 3 light out the other morning because he could not see to go to sleep?

The' local Volunteers were last Wednesday in- spected by Lord French. the Field-Marshal Commanding the Home Forces.

, This interesting event took place at the Town Hall, Chester.

The platpon turned out in strength, for the number on parade was fifty-two all told.

It is officially understood that the Rev. Tudor-Evans, the rector of Oughtrington, will in future act as the regimental chaplain.

I hear that the Lymm platoon have now got a hew marching song. It goes something like this : • Field-Marshal French he came to me and took • me by the hand, - He said to me that the Lymm platoon was the smartest in the land. Tra-la-ra-la,

• A final warning. Don't forget old Father Time this evening.

Late Mr. A.' W. Callender, J.P.—At Monday's meeting of Lymm Urban Council, Mr. W. MuHard (clerk) read a letter from Mrs. Callen- der acknowledging, with many thanks, the Council's resolution of condolence so kindly passed at their last meeting. "Will you be SCr good," she wrote, "as to convey to the Council my heartfelt thanks for your very kind expres- sion of sympathy in our overwhelming sorrow? Will you please tell them how much touched we are by their kind thought for us in pur most sad bereavement, and how greatly we appreciate their kindness and sympathy?"

===== 0002601_19160930_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTEERS AT CHESTER.

Inspected. By The Field Marshal Commanding Home Forces.

Stirring scenes were witnessed at Chester on Wednesday evening, when the members of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment were inspected by Lord French, the Field-Marshal Command- ing the Home Forces; The entire regiment were lined up in "battalions irt mess" along Northgate-street, facing the Town Hall. Soon after 6.30 the inspecting party arrived, and the regiment was called to attention, and the com- manding officers of each battalion stood at the salute while the regimental band played the regulation bars. The distinguished Field- Marshal then walked down the line and made a thorough inspection. Afterwards the entire regiment marched past in column of fours, the saluting base then being the Town Hall steps. Quite a large number of wounded, soldiers, Cadets, Scouts and veterans of the Cheshire Regiment were also inspected. The Lymm and Oughtrington platoons, which are attached to the Altrincham battalion, were amalgamated again under the command of Platoon Com- ander G. R. Williamson, with Platoon Sergeant S. D. Moston and Sergeants 'I: _I./eters lind N. Wood. The Rev. Tudor-Evans, rector of Oughtrington, who is a member of the Ougllt- rington platoon, Was in attendance as regi- mental chaplain.

THE " FIELD " DEMONSTRATION.

At Monday's meeting of Lymm Urban Council,

The Chairman (Councillor F. Lockyer); in re- ferring to the public demonstratibn in honour of Q.-M.-S. Field, D.C.M., said he wished to express his thanks to the , Council and officials and all those who took part in the procession, and also to those who so kindly acknowledged the invitation and who could not attend. He also proposed that the best thanks of the Council be conveyed .to Colonel McClure, Major Alexander, Captain Cartwright and Captain Gledhill for thfir kindness and the honour which they had paid to the Council on that occasion, which would be remembered in the days to come. (Hear, hear.) Their sincere thanks were also due to the bandmaster and the police for their kind assistance on that occasion. and he would als6. like to thank Mr._ Cowper . . _ _

and Mr. Gavin Cooke for the interest they had taken in the proceedings. Councillor Cowper thanked the Chairman for the kind manner in which he mentioned his humble services. He was always pleased to do what he could for his King and country. Councillor Gotobed said they had a day that would be looked upon in the future as a red letter day. (Applause.) They did honour to a man who deserved honour. The Chairman was to be congratulated on the arrangements that had been made. (Applause.)

EXEMPTED MEN.

The Danger Of Rushing To Munition

Works.

At the conclusion of the business of Lymm Urban Council on Monday (routine matters were disposed of), Councillor Cowper, as deputed to look after registration in the dis- trict, asked how it was that some men-in certi- fied trades who had been exempted by the Tribunal afterwards left their trades and went into munition works. In Manchester, when men were exempted, they .had to keep in the business they were in. •

Mr. Mulla-rd, the clerk, to whom the question had been put, said it was one which he could not answer.

Councillor Gotobed said they had appointed Mr. Cowper to this office, and he suggested that if he had good grounds for the statement—and he was sure he had—he should- embody them in a letter to the Chairman of the Tribunal.

Councillor Cowper said he was much obliged to Councillor Gotobed for mentioning.that, and he would do so.'

Councillor Holt said that the Tribunal grants a man conditional exemption or temporary exemption. He comes before them at the ex- piration of a few months, and• if he has shifted his occupation the circumstances are different, and he would be differently dealt with. The military authorities had got to deal with these men. They were going through these works combing out and seeing if a man was badged whether he was entitlecto it.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY TRIBUNAL.

Chairman, T. J. Ridgway; Military Repre- sentatives, Messrs. A. de C."Parmiter and 11! E. Evans.

Not Yet Released.

The case referred to at the last,meeting, in which the man applied for had been called to the Colours, came up again for hearing. The applicant (a local farmer) stated that the man had not yet been released, but he had received a letter from the military authorities at Chester stating that if he would forward them particulars as to where his unit was stationed they would endeavour to get the man trans- ferred to the Army Reserve. Stating his case for exemption, he said that he had a farm of 50 acres, 17 of which were amble. He had three horses, one foal and 1.0 head of cattle. The man was teamsman, and could do any farm work. The man was over the other week- end and said that the military authorities at his end would not even listen to him. When did he attest?—December 10th. He was most anxious not to be a conscript. , The Military Representative: He was called up because he gave his addiv,3 as Thelwall, , Thelwall then being in the Frodsham recruit- ing area.

The Chairman: Yes, but I cannot understand them keeping him. The Military Representative: He will be put in Army Reserve. Applicant: He is entitled to be put in the same positioxi, he was in prior to going to' Chester.

Adjourned until the 3rd November for man to appear.

Painter For Son.

A master, painter, plumber and decorator, who made a second appeal for his son, stated that he was his only workman. He had a lot of work on hand, and he himself could only do work that needed no climbing. Without the assistance of his son, whq was a married man with two children, he could not possibly carry on.

Mr. G. L. Welford; solicitor, representing applicant: Has the son been before the Medical Board ?—No.

Hai-e you ever tried to get labour?—Yes. I have never had a man applying for work this summer. I have offered 10th per hour, which I really could not afford to pay. I should have been out of pocket. Temporary exemption till October 20th, the Chairman remarking that before the applicant could lodge the next appeal he would have to get the consent of the Tribunal. Appeal Out Of Order.

A master painter, plumber and decorator„ who appealed for a man, stated that ye was a salaried partner, and had managed the business for some considerble time.

Mr. G. L. Welford, who represented_ appli- cant, in apologising to the Tribunal, stated that the member of the firm who intended to be present had been called away on important business, and was unable to attend. In the cir- cumstances he would ask the Tribunal to grant an adjournment. (This was the second appeal.) The Clerk pointed out that the present appeal form was signed by the firm, in the firm's namb, and the previous one was signed in the man's name. The present appeal was, therefore, out of order, and could not be consideied. The Chairman: The only way that we could put it right is to grant an adjournment and give him leave to re-sign it. The Tribunal granted an adjournment until October 6th, and gave leave to amend the appeal. " Coal-bagging Trade.

A coal merchant who appealed for his son, stated that he bought the business for his sons five 'years ago. He had two sons, but one suffered ill-health, and recently had to leave the business owing to the work being too heavy. He had 150, customers to serve weekly, which represent an average of about! 15 tons weekly. The coal was taken from house to house in bags. The weight of each bag of coal was slightly over lcwt. Does your client work?—Yes. The doctor told him to do light work. He has twice burst blood vessels. The Military Representative: Have you any medical evidence to this effect?—No! He was, very reluctant about telling me. Why? There •is no disgrace in bursting blood vessels; -

Adjourned "till October 6th for the man to go before Medical Board.

Wounded Soldiers Entertain School Children. —The wounded soldiers from the Brookfield Hospital entertained a large number of school children in the Drill Hall last Saturday after- noon. They gave a repeat performance of the previous t Wednesday's splendid concert (slightly curtailed), which was thoroughly enjoyed. The children from the Lymm and Oughtrington Schools were specially invited, and the wounded soldiers from the 'Raddon Court Hospital also attended. Each child d ,pai a penny for admission, and by these means be- tweeri four and five pounds was added to the already magnificent sum. Great credit is due to Sergeant Corcoran (the organiser) and his merry men for their splendid effort.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH OF FORMER TERRITORIAL.

Air. H. Rigby Succumbs To Injuries

Sustained At Work.

It is with the deepest regret that we announce the death of ex-Private Herbert Rigby, a well- known time-expired Territorial. He was fol- lowing his employment at the Partington Steel and Iron \Co., Irlam, last week-end, when a large portion of red hot slag fell on the top of ,him and a fellow workmate. His workmate suc- cumbed to the severe burns the same morning, and despite the best attention rendered by the officials of the Manchester Royal Infirmary, Mr. Rigby succumbed early on Thursday morning. The news will be received with sorrow by our lads "out there," for up to a short time ago he was one of their popular comrades. During the present crisis he has about two years' service to his credit, sixteen months of which have been spent in France. He was 24 years of age, single, and resided with his parents at Rush Green. He was a brave and noble lad, always willing to de a good turn, and exceedingly popular. His fellow victim was a married man with two children.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE THOMAS MOSTON- ILL IN HOSPITAL.

Private Thomas Moston, of the Royal Scots, has been admitted to hospital suffering from a general breakdown. In a letter received by some relatives in Statham, it is stated that he had ,been admitted into hospital, but at the time of writing he was feeling a good deal better. Private Thomas Moston resides with parents at Rush Green. He enlisted in the Royal Scots in February, 1915, and was drafted out to Egypt in August of the same year. After serving some month's in this Eastern clime, he was sent out to France with anothei battalion, arriving there in May of this year. His battalion took part in the big advance, and at the time he became ill they were on the banks of the Somme. Prior to the war he was employed as a butcher at Northenden. He was educated at the .Lymm School and wor- shipped at the Congregational Church.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====

LANCE-CORPORAL T. FAIRHURST. Killed.

of myself and the officers of B Company to ex- press our sorrow and our sincere sympathy with you over,the loss of your gallant son. He was a good soldier and N.C.0., and it was my in- tention on leaving this part to put him up for a second Stripe. He died doing his duty, and I think this thought will help to alleviate the bitter grief of his loss.—With deep sympathy, I remain, yours truly, E. 0. Crosfield." Lance-Corporal Fairhurst was 24 years of age, and was an old member of the South Lancs. Regiment, having been with them four years previous to the war. When war broke out he felt it his duty to join the Colours. After train- ing for a few months he finally crossed over to France on the 12th February, 1915: He had but one leave, which was last November. Always bright and of a cheerful disposition, he was be- loved of all who knew him. He was born in Grappenhall, and was educated under Mr. Evans at the Grappenhall School, and was also a member of the Grappenhall Independent Methodist Sunday School and Chapel.

PAD GATE.

Wesley Guild Social.—The opening social of the Fesley Guild for the winter session was held on Thursday evening, presided over by the Rev. A. J. Morton. In a ishort address to the members he mentioned that we were passing thrpugh the greatest epoch of any history that hag ever been made, and woe betide any Church who is not alive to its liabilities. We ought not to be careless and listless, but we want to keep the spiritual things of life fore- most, all pleasures to have second place. Songs were very ably sung by Miss Vera Jones and Mrs. Hazeldine, and recitations were given by Mrs. Nurse. Progressive games, musical chairs, &c., were played. Refreshments were served during the interval. The accompanists were Mrs. Cottrell, Mrs. W. J. Bate, and Mr. Wads- worth.

Presentation to Miss H. Bennett.—A social evening qrganised by the past and present scholars of the Senior Girls' Class Of the Pad- gate Wesleyan Sunday School was held on Saturday evening in the Schoolroom. As a mark of respect and appreciation of services rendered, a presentation was made during the evening to Miss H. Bennett, Bruche Heath, who has resigned her position as teacher of the class, having been connected with Sunday school work at Padgate as scholar and teacher practically all her life. The presentation took the form of a silver inkstand, pen and nickel reading lamp. Mrs. Charles Healy, one of the' old members of the class, who handed over the gift, spoke of the length and kind of service which Miss Bennett had rendered and ex- pressed regret that she had been compelled to relinquish her position and wished that the resignation had only been temporary. Miss Bennett very suitably replied, expressing her appreciation of the kindly thought which had led the members to -.make her such a useful present. The rest of, the evening's proceedings, which were of a social nature, were presided over by Mrs. Dakin. The soloists were Miss B. London, Miss N. Appleton, Mrs. W. J. Bate and Mrs. Dakin, and recitations were given by Miss E. Holt and Miss H. Thring. Games were also played during the. evening and refresh- ments were provided.,

Parish Council.—Mr. W. Mclntyre, chairman, presided at Tuesday's meeting a the Stockton Heath Parish. Council. The customary pay- ments were authorised, and no matter of unusual interest appeared on the agenda.

PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

Warrington And The Disposal Of The Village Refuse.

Mr. G. Pilling Presided at the meeting of Stockton Heath Parochill Committee on Tues- day. Mr. Hough (clerk) reported in connection with the disposal of night refuse that he had communicated with the Town Clerk of War- rington asking if the Corporation would take the six loads weekly as suggested. From an in- timation he got from the Deputy Town Clerk he. understood that the Corporation would try to do what was asked. The contractor had told him that up to the present they had been taking ten. Mr. 'Westbrook thought' this very satisfactory, and moved that the Corporation be_thanked for their consideration.

Mr. Pearson seconded, and the Chairman re- marked that he had great pleasure in putting the resolution to the meeting.

Mr. Hough was requested to convey the reso- lution to the Town Clerk.

Stockton Heath And Grappen- Jiall Volunteers inspected.

„ The Stockton Heath and GrappenbAn De- tachments of the V.T.C, being the 4th Com- pany of the 6th Battalion (Knutsford) of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment, attended the inspection by Lord French at Chester on Wednesday afternoon. _

The Stockton Heath Detachment numbered about 80 and were under the command of Platoon Commander F. Warburton; while- the Grappenhali contingent, under Platoon Com- mander F. A. Frost, paraded about 20! strong. Leaving Warrington by the 4.22 p.m.. train, the company, on arrival- in Chester,. marched from the general station to the Town Hall amd formed up on the roadway in line With the other detachments of the regiment. Lord French arrived at 6.30; and, having inspected the regiment, the Company 'Commanders were called out and the Field Marshal addressed a few congratulatory remarks, on their smart ap- pearance,. speaking in almost the same terms. as he did at Liverpool. The regiment then marched past in companies in column- of route and the parade terminated. The day was fine and bracing and the men enjoyed themselves very much. - The local contingents returned to Warrington at 8.15.

PENKETH.

Death of Mr. Geerge Davies.—On Tuesday another well-known member of the community passed away, after a very short illness, in the person of Mr. George Davies, Warrington-road. In February he attained his seventieth birth- day, but has not lived long enough to receive his national allowance. Originally a file cutter, he at various times followed several careers. He possessed the - long service medal of the old Volunteers, which he joined in Warrington very shortly after their inception. As an ardent member of that force he won badges and dis- tinctions, worthy mementoes of a worthy object. He was also a well-known bass singer, having sung in various churches in town before he removed to the Sankey and Penketh district, where he became a prominent member of the Sankey Church Choir, which position he held for nearly thirty years. The Musical Society also claimed him as a member. For the last twenty-five years he has been official lamp- lighter, walking fourteen miles each day, seven miles lighting and seven turning out.. A widow and four daughters are left to mourn his loss.

ROSES AND WAR..

Raising Funds For The District Working Party.

The Penketh Rose Society recently held a rose show for the purpose of raising money for the War Relief Fund. The attempt has succeeded wonderfully well, for 21 18s. has been handed over to the secretary of the Penketh and Sankey Working Party. The winners at the rose show were Mr. J. Ray; 2, Mr. J. Appleton; 3, Mr. J. Morris; 1, Mr. J. Davies.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
Work The Pai-ty Is Doing.

The following is a copy of a letter from the Hon. Treasurer of the Working Party, sent to the employes of the Penketh Tanning C0.:.-" I have to-night received your note and cheque for £lO for the funds of the Penketh and Sankey Working Party, for which I enclose receipt. it is very generous of the employes to help us so much. As far as possible parcels are sent to every man that goes from the village. If any are missed out it is simply that their names are not given in. The women members of the working party have been asked to bring names and addresses to the Mission on Thursdays. We have now sent 196 parcels to men. In many cases more socks have been Aent to village men who require them. I enclose a balance sheet. Full particulars of accounts can be seen at the Mission on the Thursday we meet, also a list of the boys to whom parcels have been sent. If you would like the list I could send it by Mr. Woods; then if you found any boys had -been left out it ;could be rectified. We have also pent a self-propelling chair to Winwick. Of colirse, at present the hospitals get a great amount of ciur work. If anyone who visits WinwiCk cares to look on the back of the chairs they will find one with a brass tablet on, saying it is from the Penketh and Sankey Working Party. Each chair costs £4 Bs. pd., and that was collected in small amounts from the workers. Thanking you and the employes for their generous support of our work.—Yours truly, Harriet Gilbert, hon. treasurer."

Seventy-two soldiers are expected to arrive bn Tuesday at the Runcorn Workhouse, part of which has been converted into a military hos- pital. RAILWAY DISPUTE.

War Bonus Increased By Five Shillings.

An agreement has been reached on the ques- tion of the railwaymen's demand ,for an in- crease of wages to meet the increased cost of living. The basis of the agreement is that the previous war bonus of ss. for employes of 18 and upwards is to be increased to 10s., ar d that an extra 2s. 6d. is given to those whose previous bonus was 2s. 6d.—employes under 18, with cer- tain exceptions.
