CHAPTER XVLII.

Waiting. in, the row-boat, Quabba heard Esther's scream, as she lumped from the yacht to avoid Blair Stanleigh. As she came to the surface the girl. grasped the rail of the boat, and Quabba drew her in and. headed the boat straight for the beach. On. deck Blair and Luke were fighting viciously,. It was a splendid, smashing scrap, and the sailors thoroughly enjoyed, the spectacle. Like maddened beasts the two men tore at each other, and the snowy deck of the yacht was stained with blood. Against the panelled mahogany of the cabin side the fighters crashed, smashing and splintering glass and woodwork_ And then, in sheer tearing rage, combining all their strength to dash each other to insensibility and death, they 'plunged from the smashed. cabin side at the centre to the stanchion rail. At the heavy impact of the two strong bodies:flung against it the rail bent outward and the stanchions snapped like pipe-stems, and the two. men locked in death grips-, went over the side and. into the water, kicking, thrusting, cursing. In the watbr, the more activetßlair loosened hold and dived under. Coming to the surface, he waited for the giPAY's head to appear; and when Luke rose to the surfate, bubbling and gasping for breath, Blair struck him a smashing blow between the eyes and swam to the companion-way. Half- stunned.. and half-strangled, Luke screamed in inarticulate insanity of rage and hate. Then through his dull mind came a realisation that Blair, as friend of the owner of the yacht and as "a gentleman," would have the aid and assistance of the sailors. Leaving the matter for another day to settle, ,for Luke was of a patine that loved to nurse and cherish his enmity,. the gipsy turned and swam strondly and doggedly to the shore.

Blair,. panting, water: soaked, and dishevelled, leaned over the rope railing of the companion-way and looked after Luke, also with that respect one good fighting man has for another. "Hurt, sir?" a sailor asked. "Only a little scratched," said Blair, noting with some satisfaction that the cold seawater had checked the bleeding of his wounds.

"The. poor fellow must have gone roaring mad," added Blair in explanation. "We had been lost in the woods for some days, and he must have had some whisky and gone clean crazy, for the first thing I 4 knew he sprang upon me, fighting like a mad dog!"

It was this same explanation that Blair made to the sailing master when the latter returned in the launch with the sailors from their shore leave. For the two sailors haft in the forecastle had not seen or known of Esther's boarding the yacht in search of Arthur, or her wild leap from it when faced by Blair and Luke, nor had the sailing master known of her presence aboard the yacht. The sailing master had seen the row-boat with Quabba and Esther headed straight for the beach, but the row- boat was too far away for even keen sailor eyes to note that the girl was wet and weak. "I have heard of sailors getting homicidal d.t.'s," said the sailing master, "but I never knew a landsman to get 'em that quick." The two sailors who had witnessed the Titan combat grinned, for it had been a bout to delight the eye. But just then the sailing master gave a sudden cry. "Look at that fool! What is he doing? Trying to commit suicide?" and the master pointed to the ,beach, where the row-boat, with the tired, unskilled hands of Quabba on the oars, was negotiating the surf.

"Shall we lower a boat?" asked one of the sailors. "There was a woman spilled from the boat with that awkward land lubber !" The sailing master shaded his eyes with his hand and said, "By George, the girl can swim all right She is even helping the land lubber. They're all right. See, the fellow with her is ask- ing someone to save the boat. There is quite a little bunch of people on the beach now." Not only were Esther and Quabba safe ashore, but Luke had gained a deserted part of the beach unobserved, shook the water from him, and strode off.

Luke Lovell had no use for the town. Far away in the Sierras the Lady Veronica mines and other prosperous and busy diggings called him. By train and on foot he made his way, and by another day Santa Barbara was far behind him. But though he went for work, and to get away from his very hate of Blair Stanleigh, he had not deviated one iota from his set, sullen purpose. He had seen the paper that Blair Stanleigh had pur- sued and persecuted Esther to obtan. Then, too, Luke had beheld The Diamond from the Sky. Luke clenched his hand and looked drown as though he expected to see the great gem he had thrice grasped as his own, only to lose, still burning bale- fully in his bronzed and brawny grasp. But the Diamond was gone, and he clutched at the empty air and trudged on, resolved that the purpose of his life would be to have it. But he would have no more of Blair Stanleigh; that he was resolved. Away at the Lady Veronica Mine there was ex- citement. The driver of the trap had been suc- coured by ranchers. Brought back, he told the " boss " of the attack made upon them. The fore- man acted at once. The girl had come to the mines to find John Powell, and twice had been attacked. "The master must know of this," he said to his wife, and he used the long-distance telephone to get into communication with Arthur. oxe)neceneeeece

I'm taking the fastest car and I'm making for the mines !" At his office Arthur dashed like a madman frora- the telephone, crying excitedly to his secretary he went that a most urgent matter called him to, the mines, and ran from the building to his wait- ing motor car.

Scarcely had he left when a wire was received ab the office from Esther: "Came here to see you on urgent business. Wire instructions." The only thing the secretary could do was to wire that Mr. Powell had left for the mines seeking her. There was nothing now to be done, but return to, the mines, and Esther and Quabba too train to the nearest station and engaged horses for the remainder of the journed.

It was pay-day at Mammoth, a noisy mining settlement. Mr. Huff was the proprietor of " Pete's Palace," the one amusement and 'public acooninlo- dation enterprise. It was a dance hall, a bar, and a restaurant combined. Money flowed like water at Mammoth, which was in the centre of the newly-opened mines in the mountains, where johu Powell and other magnates were putting in more money than perhaps they ever would get out. But .things were at a lull when Frank Durand, "King of Diamonds," arrived. He had received word from some underground source that several of the train robbers who had escaped after the hold-up in which The Diamond from the Sky was taken from a woman passenger—Vivian—consorted at " Pete's Palace" at Mammoth. A few signs in the under-world fraternal code and Pete and Durand were in conference. A few Words over- heard by a hulking zipsy fellow at the bar, and third person was party to the conversation. This person was Luke Lovell, who, retracing his stePs in search of • work, had encountered at Mammoth the wandering desert Indian. In him Luke had proof to substantiate to Durand his story of the diamond.

"It's at the bottom of the sea," concluded Luke, gruffly. •

" Pete's Palace" filled up, the dance was at its height. He had not raised his eyes at the door when there was an outbreak at the counter that. attracted the attention of all. A man, who, was a' beast had grasped the shrinking Esther:, saYit'g : "Come and dance." Even a life such as the woman wee leading could not stamp out the desire to protect the innocent and motherless. Great tears coursed down her cheeks as, turning in fury upon Pete' she said, "This is a mother's prayer. The locket is that poor girl's." As though this were a signal at which all demoniac passions were to break loose, the dance hall viragos, in an outburst of emotion and frenzied anger, sprang upon the proprietor of the place end all his bullies, scratching and biting. For one brief moment Arthur and Esther gazed into each other's eye, and for the first and alas! for the last time fOr many bitter days, were clasped each other's arms. And then pandemonium reigneal Instinctively all took sides, the good elemellk against the bad, and even the better element ef the bad against the worst. But where was The Diamond from the Sky? (To be continued.)

TWENTY POINTS 10 NIL.

`^ The Linnets" Have Their Feathers Clipped At •Wilderspool. Warrington soundly drubbed Runcorn at Wilderspool ,by beating them by 20 points to nothing. This was how the score mounted pp: First Half: Tries. Goals. Jylley - Baker

Harmer Baker Second Half:

T ranter Jolley Douglas Tranter

Dumbell 0 Dumbell ... 1 Matthews ... 0 Dumbell 0

Quite an interesting and convincing list!

• When the teams trickled on to the field the crowd of about 2,000 took the measure of °them, and Warrington's advantage was • apparent. Most et the Runcorn players were very youth- ful, ,like raw recruits, and the only old stal- warts among them were J. Jolley, Okell, 'Richa,rdsort and Burgess. There was a vast difference between the Runcorn team and that which was graced by Knoxy Houghton and Jimmy Jolley. On the other hand, Warring- ton were in the happy position of blending well and truly tried and young players, and the com- bination was effective. Knowing the, quality they were 'up against, they forced the game greatly in the first half, with the result that in the second half not a few of the visitors suffered from weariness of physique and, as the impos- sibility of their winning became more pro- nounced, weariness of spirit. The interval also was too long and, on a hot day, this always tends to produce lassitude. Xhey were handi- capped, moreover, by the leave-taking of Nickson, who, when tackling Jolley, sustained a bad cut on the left eye.

'One was glad to mitice that the taking of goal-kicks was not confined to Jolley, for in seasons when we have had one wan repeatedly taking them ' and, When he has not been playing, there was no one else. who could act as a good substitute. The development of this branch ,pf the art generally is all to the good, and, judging 13 the shots. on Saturday, is much needed, for only one goal was scored, though, of course, most of the attempts were made from difficult angles.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
The forwards were very good, and hung to- gether well. Matched against them, however, was not much weight, and they indulged in some rushes which reminded one of the days of the Shugars—Boardman--Thomas combina- tion. These onslaughts are worth cultivating. In this department there is no doubt that War- rington is well served.

-- Coming to the half-backs, Douglas and Callon, though they played hard, were not quite up to Young and Nicholas in the match against Broughton, and one Was pleased to observe a smartening up of the game after the interval, when Young changed places with Douglas. ,Immediately the former took up position at half the game moved much more quickly. If Young has to "join up" it will be a serious loss to Warrington, because he is far and away the " classiest " man inothe team.

• Among the three-quarters Baker was at his best, and it was rather a pity that the move- ment which started from the half-way line and culminated in his running round the post should have been negatived through an obstruction by one of the forwards and, though it was not appreciated by the majority of the crowd, one -• may say that it was a legitimate decision. His would have 'been a glorious first try but for that. It is as well that spectators should be reminded that there are laws of the game. Tranter was again a tower of strength to the team—his flutter with Salford has done him no' harm! A pretty swift and strong runner— Euston to Glasgow express style, tearing up the Permanent way—his chief asset is his brain. Re has a mind for football. -Re can take in a situation at a glance, and makes tracts for deal- ing with it while others are merely thinking •••-• over it. This showed up particularly on one occasion, when Runcorn threatened danger. } Four of the visitors were racing along and pass- ing excellently. Seeing them, Tranter set hin- self to break up the movement by getting in such a position that the man with the ball had either to part with it and lose it as well as his mates, or, if he stuck to it, remain within easy reach of Tranter. The ball wa loosed and Tranter scooped it up and danger was averted. One recollects that Tranter was introduced to ',Warrington -as a forward, but his initiative so distinguished him that the committee were soon persuaded to shift him to the' three-quarter line. In the forward line he was good stuff wasted, although he had always played in that position in the works' competition. A strong lad, he has a terrific and irresistible rush with him—a "Tank," as it were.

It was Britton's first spell with Warrington, and, obviously, the lad was nervous and, to make matters worse for him, he did not receive much sympathy and encouragement from the spectators. It does not aid a young .player tf) hear shouts of, "Push him in the pack." It 9ight have been better for him had, he been flaying away .first, because, at home, the spec- tators watch every movement and are quick to detect error. But under the circumstances, Britton did well. A sure judgment of his ability is impossible from orie match, brit one thinks him worthy of an extended trial. At

===== 0002601_19160930_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
INCIDENTS OF TRE MATCH,

Coming to the details of the game, Runcorn won the toss and set the home team to play against a strong wind and sun. Play became furious and on the centre line Harmer had to make his mark to stem a great rush by the whole of the Runcorn forwards. Warrington forged ahead against the wind, and frbm a scrummage Young got possession and raced through several men, but spoiled a good chance by a forward pass. Tranter getting the ball was within a few strides of passing the Runcorn line, but Jack Jolley brought his man down and saved grandly. Warrington still pressed and Matthews, Dumbell and Ward looked like scoring, but Flanagan spoilt their efforts on the last lap and touched down: Runcorn cot the ball away and were travelling nicely when an infringement of rules gave Warrington a free-kick at the half-way line and the game once more raged under the visitors' cross-bar. A good kick by Jolley relieved the pressure .and midfield play followed, both sides showing sterling form. NicksOn and Hailwood for Runcorn were very keen and took a deal of watch- ing to prevent a break through. Okell and Rich- ardson were next prominent for Runcorn and the Warrington line was for the first time in danger. The ball was kicked away, but Runcorn returned and kept Warrington on the defence..; Baker got the ball and was on the point of scoring, but Glegg obstructed and the chance was missed. Ben Jolley was called on to clear his lines, which he did • handsomely, and al scrummage took place in Runcorn territory, the whole of the forwards endeavouring to carry the ball over. A free-kick for off-side against the home lot brought relief, but F. Jolley, the Runcorn back, failing to gather the ball a minute later, Baker and Britton rushed for the line and looked like getting over safely, when Burgess rushed up and just got the ball into touch at the corner. The home Jolley tried to get through, but was grounded. Still the play hovered about the Runcorn goal. Jolley and then Tranter tried this forcing game, but Runcorn were not to be had and tackled so keenly that individual play did not succeed. Jolley then tried other tactics, making his mark near the centre line and finding touch well into the Runcorn half. Runcorn came again and Lightfoot nearly got through, Ben Jolley checking him in good time. A grand passing movement by the whole Warriugton quartet of threequarters was spoiled by Britton passing forward. Harmer scored the first try for Warrington during e melee on the Runcorn line, Jolley failing to add the goal. Just 'on half-time F. Jolley missed his kick, and Baker, taking prompt advantage, scored. Jolley again missed at goal. Half-time: Warrington, 2 tries (6 points); Run- corn, nil. Nickson, who had cut his face in collision with the home full-back, did not turn out again, and Runcorn, handicapped by his absence, the wind, and the sun, had but a poor prospect. Warring- ton were almost immediately pressing and Young made a bid for the line, but was brought down. In less than five minutes Ben Jolley took the ball past several opponents, passed to Tranter, and the latter had little difficulty in crossing the line, thus scoring try number three. Dumbell took the kick, but missed the goal. Ben Jolley tried to score once again and this time succeeded in such an easy position that Dumbell easily added the goal. After that Douglas and Tranter added unconverted tries in capital style. Result :—Warrington, 1 goal 6 tries (20 points); Runcorn, nil. The teams were :

WARRINGTON: B. Jolley; Baker, Tranter, Britton, Young; Douglas, Callon; Harmer, Cunliffe, Glegg, Matthews, Dumbell, Ward. RUNCORN: F. Jolley; Walker, Flanagan, Light- foot, J. Jolley; Peacock, Okell; Richardson, Bur- gess, Spruce, Booth, Hailwood, Nickson. .

THE IMMORTAL JACK FISH.

" ' Wirepuller ' writes me," says Forward in the "Athletic News," "from a training camp, and asks me to assist him in providing a few facts for a discussion. The point at issue is the merits of John Fish and the late James Leytham. A North-Country soldier in the tent declared that the Wigan player was 'the finest footballer who ever Played 'for the Northern Union.' 'Wire- puller ' naturally put forth the claims of 'Jack ' Fish, of Warrington. "Without in any way favouring one side or the other, I might say that both were great players, each with a distinct but none the less effective style of his own. However, ' Wirepuller ' wants some Nets about Fish, so here they are. "The' Warrington man's best season was in 1900-1, when for his club he scored 167 points was, for his county 43. His best county score that season was three goals and five tries against Northumberland and Durham. Several times Fish scored five tries in one match for Warrington, and his club record was eight goals and five tries (31 points) for Warrington against Huddersfield in a Northern League match' on October 20th, 1906.

_ I may tell my correspondent that, in addition to his football skill, Fish was a fine runner, and that in one season in track racily-, he took £57 in prizes. The N.C.A.A. Suspended him as an ama- teur because of his connection with Northern Union football."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 30, 1916.

ASSOCIATION.

(Lancashire Combination.)

THE TOWN'S REVENGE.

South Beaten By One Goal On o*n

Ground.

As the Town had visited the Dingle Park 'enclosure the previous Saturday, and remem- bering the exciting game then witnessed which resulted in a victory for South Liverpool by the only goal of the match, their meeting on Satur- day was looked forward to with great interest. There did not appear, however, any likelihood of the South meeting with a reverse, but the Town players were full of confidence, ankl won by two goals to one.

South Liverpool fielded ,practically the same side as on the previous Saturday; but the Town made a few changes, Whitfield, Haywood and Stephens being included. Warrington lost the toss and started down hill with plenty of dash, and it was arly in evidence that they meant to make certain of victory, and the front line ' being well supported by the halves, set up a violent bombardment, and had it not been for the soundness of the home defence the Town would have been through more than once in the early stages of the game. Some pretty open play followed, both sets of forwards taking part, and it was clearly a "forwards' game." The 'defence of both sides seemed unbeatable, and for a time play was in midfield, until Hay- wood, bluffing his Opponents, raced along, pass- ing to Owen, who centred and Aspinnll, beat- ing man after Man, put in a splendid shot, which gave Moore absolutely no chance.

This roused the spirit of South Liverpool, and they swept to the attack with determination, but Leah and Millward were tackling strongly, and Kite was as safe as ever. The half-backs, more than holding th4ir own, returned the ball into Liverpool's quarters. Several shots were tried, but without result. The Town front line was a constant source of danger to the home defence, A tpinall especially playing a great game. Behind, the halves were a determined lot, Whitfield playing a "brainy " game, Hay- wood brilliant, with Stephens making. an effective debut.

About 'five minutes from the interval the Town were again rewarded for their strenuous efforts, Aspinall again working his way through and giving his side the lead by two goals at the interval.

The first-period of the game had provided a vigorous tussle, yet full of good football, the sides were equally balanced, the Town making the best of their opportunities and turning round with confidence commenced the second half up-hill arid facing a strong sun. South Liverpool at once made great strides for Kite. Time and again they did all they could to get through, but the Town were determined and always kept a good grip of their opponents, Ditchfield, as usual, working like a Trojan and very excellently trying some of his first-timers, but without success.

Owen was twice put out of action and received first-aid—the second time after a fine run and a shot which deserved success, but which struck the upright awl went out of play. Taylor was exceeaingly weak, and requires training. Illidge seems to have got rid of his selfish methods, and a marked improvement was noticeablc. He is deserving of further encouragement. About fifteen minutes from time, after a scramble in front of the Town goal, South Liverpool scored, ,the rest of the play was up and down, the South making, every endeavour to find an equaliser, but the end of a great and exciting game saw the Town the victors after 90 minutes of thoroughly interesting football.

It is something to boast of to gain a victory at Dingle Park. South Liverpool, when wtn- ning the toss, invariably play up hill first halt, and when thus favoured have never been beaten. Their suporters are eager for another game to be played at Liverpool.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
ECCLES BOROUGH AT HOOD LANE TO-DAY.

After an absence of two weeks at Liverpool, War- rington Town are to play on their own ground at Hood-lane to-day (Saturday), their opponents being Eccles Borough. A good game is anticipated. Last season perhaps the best match at Hood-lane' was that between the Town and Eccles, when we were the victctrs by three goals to nothing—a triumph minimised by a subsequent defeat. of one gal to nothing at Eccles. A few weeks 'ago, it will be recollected, Leigh, of Eccles, was a wing man with Siddall for Warrington against Tranmere Rovers. but his own team. Eccles, have claimed him, and to-day he will be among the opposition. Last season Siddall played for them, and it will be par- ticularly interesting to-day to watch his play. Warrington's team will be selected from: Kite; Leah, Millward ; Stephens. Whitfield, Ditchfield: Illidge, Aspinall, Kendrick, Siddall, Giblin and Owen.

Heywood, who played for the Town last week, has gone to the Front.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT JUNIOR LEAGUE.

P. W. L. D. F. A. PTS. Howley St. Elphin's Bewsey A.S. Penketh juniors Latchford Recs. Orford Juniors .1 Highfield Juniors Latchford Cadets

HIGHFIELD JUNIORS v. BEWSEY A.S.

On the around of the former club.

Highfield : J. Thornhill; Worrall, Jepson; Wish- lade. Hankey, Barrow; Dunne, Moulsdale, Bell, Ford, Tomlinson. Bewsey A.S. : Perks; Atkinson, Main; Worrall, James, Dickinson; Hassall, Roylance, Payton, Bradburn, Schofield. Referee, Mr. P. Gosling.

The visitors won the toss and played with a good breeze at their backs. Bewsey were seen to the best advantage, and after ten minutes' play they opened the score. The home forwards played more keenly, but were opposed by a stubborn defence and play was transferred to the home quarters; and after Thornhill and his backs had been (severely tested they again had to yield their fortress to the visitors. In spite of these reverses the home team play,' very pluckily and on several occasions were unfortunate with good shots that were a shade too wide or too high. The visitors scored again before the interval, when the score was :—Bewsey. 3 goals; Highfield, 0. The home team now having the wind in their favour were fully expected to decrease their arrears, and they at once set a fast pace and put the visitors on the defensive. There was no holding the home forwards and Bell scored after five minutes' play. Moulsdale scored a second goal, and five minutes later Ford made the scores equal. Now came the tug-of-war for the winning point, and both teams played strenuously. Each end was quickly visited and the backs were de- fending strongly under pressure, but the N; isitors I appeared to have the better staying poweri, and during an attack a few minutes before the dose of play the home backs blundered and let in thei visitors' forwards. They made the best use of the opening and gained' the winning goal. 'Result: Bewsey A.S., 4 goals; Highfield, 3 goals.

PENKETH JUNIORS v. ORFORD JUNIORS. On the ground of the former. An early visit was paid to the visitors' defensive lines. The visitors retaliated, but found the , home defence too gopd. The game Continued to be 'evenly contested, and each end was visited 'without result, and at half- time no score had been registered. The second half opened very vigorously, with the home team more in evidence, and, owing to a mis- take by the' visitors' defenders, Pearson broke through and scored for the home team. The visitors replied strenuously,- but failed to break down the home defence. Moss and Thornasson displaying very good form. No further scoring took place, and the, home team gained a hard-earned victory by one goal to nil.

In the Junior League the- games last week provided some keen encounters, and victories were equally divided between the home and visiting clubs,' and ii—good crop of goals wa,. secured, the winners claiming thirteen and the losers six. .

The game between the Highfield and Bewsey teams; who were opposed to each other for the first time in a League engagement, was very keenly contested, the home team being very eager to gain their initial points, but at the interval the visitors had gained a substantial lead .of three goals, and it looked very gloomy for the home supporters; but after the chang,, of ends the home players cheered their droop- ing spirits by playing in the very best style, and after they had scored their first goal the Bewsey defence ~vaS found plenty of work to do, and eventually the scores were made equal. It was at this, pciint that both teams played with fresh vigour, and the visitors proved dui stronger lot. During a stern attack on. the home goal the backs blundered and the visiters quickly availed themselves of the opening and snatched the victory in the closing...,minutes of the game.

The Latchford Cadets and the Latchford Rees were also in opposition for the first time in League struggles, and the Cadets, who had pre- viously met with defeat' on their own ground, were.out to gain their first League victory, but they had built their castles in the air and their hopes were again Shattered, defeat being their share: Four•.goals were scored ' against their solitary one, and they now occupy bottom position with eighteen goals against and only two goals for.

It is evident the Cadets require some shoo t- i ng practice.

Bradshaw, on his first appearance, played very well, and scored the Cadets only goal. Greenhalgh and Massey also did \well. The Recs. were best served by Brandwood, Green, Willcock and Heesom.

The game on the Penketh Rec. between the Juniors and Orford proved a very keen and 1.,r- -teresting contest, and the result being oxe goal against nil in favour of the home team was very satisfactory to the supporters of both clubs, of whom there was a ,very good number present. Throughout the ga'mo the, defenet, proved equal to all demands made by the opposing forwards, with the exception of a mis- take by the Orford backs,, which allowed Pear- son to nip in and snatch the only goal for the home club.

HOWLEY UNITED v. ST. ELPHIN'S

The 'meeting of the above strong rivals in the Victoria Park wa the means *pi attracting a large crowd. . _

Howley United: Walters; Foster, Rowlinson; Hoarty, Miller,- Atherton; Humphries, Woodhouse, Davies. Shalieshaft, Boyle. '

St. Blp—hin's; Dor'rian'; Furness, Tunstall; Buck- ley, Miller, Cooper; Chisholm, England, Jones, Watson, Holden. Referee, Mr. S. C. Essenhigh. .

The United won the toss and Jones set the ball rolling, and by strong play the Saints' goal was quickly placed in danger. Good play by Tunstall and Furness relieved the situation, and even play followed. Good dribbling by, Woodhouse in mid- field enabled the home forwards to lead an attack on the Saints' goal, and Shakeshaft, receiving a good pass from Boyle, opened the score for the United. The Saints straightaway made a keen assault on the United goal, a corner resulting. This was well placed by Chisholm, and Walters had to concede another corner to save his goal from being cap- tured. This time Holden placed well in front of goal, and Walters fisting out, Miller (Saints) re- ceived and returned the leather quickly and made the scores equal, Walters making a splendid effort. to clear. The game was now contested in strenuous fashion, and many fine struggles took place between Miller (United) and Jones, the Saints' centre-for- ward. Gradually the United gained the upper hand, and came to close grips with the Saints' defence. During a scramble Davies gained the lead for the United. Following this midfield play was the order until the Saints were able to resume the offensive by the aid of a free kick. Holden, re- ceiving the ball when well placed, shot hard at goal. and from Walter's clearance Jones secured and again made the scores equal. A little later Wood- house made several futile efforts to regain the lead, but the interval arrived with the scores being 2 goals each. The second portion was resumed in ding-dong style, and keen tackling was frequent, several players receiving minor injuries., Walters was suc- cessful in dealing with a hot shot from Jones, and then play was transferred to the Saints' goal and a penalty kick was awarded to the United for a trip by Tunstall. Davies was entrusted with the kick and placed the ball safely between the uprights, Dorrian being well beaten. The Saints now tried hard to equalise again, but Miller, at centre-half for the United, was playing a strong game, and broke up many of the Saints' attacks. Play veered to the Saints' quarters and Shakeshaft centring well, Woodhouse placed the home team further ahead with a capital shot The Saints replied in spirited fashion, and Foster cleared well when hard pressed. Rowlinson had to retire for a while owing to an injury, and Woodhouse fell to the back position, and was prominent in defeating the visitors' fe;r- wards on many occasions. Chisholm scored for the Saints, but was ruled to be in an off-side position. The players now began to show signs of fatigue. The pace slowed down and Igo further goals were scored. Result: Howley rnited, 4 goals; St. Elphin's, 2 goals. For the Howley team, Walters in goal gave a good exhibition, and was ably assisted by. Foster and Rowlinson. Miller was' the best half-back on the field. Woodhouse was the outstanding forward on the day's play, arid was ably supported by Shakeshaft, Davies and Humphries. For the Saints, Tunstall and Furness kicked well when hard pressed, H. Miller being the best of a moderate middle line, and Jones, Holden and Eng- land were the pick of the forward line.

THE PRIZES PRESENTED.

At the conclusion of. the games 'Mrs. Frost pre- sented the prizes to the successful competitors.

In asking her to do so, Mr. Locker thanked Mrs. Frost for attending, and said the games had been well fought, and he was sure the winners would treasure the prizes which they received at the hands of Mrs. Frost.

Presenting the prizes, Mrs. Frost said she was proud to come there that afternoon. She had never been able to play bowls herself, but she thought she had now better begin to do so in her old age. (Laughter.) In congratulating the winning team and sympathising with the losers, sl3e said the games had been a pleasure to watch and she would like to give a prize herself next• year, if someone would only '" jog " her memory. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. W. Murray proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Frost, who, he said, had so willingly come forward to perform that duty. She was a lady of energy and always did what sh‘ could for the general good. (Applause.) Mr. E. Wallington seconded, and Mrs. Frost suitably replied.

MONKS, HALL & CO. v. BRUNSWICK VILLA. Efforts are being made at the Monks, Hall & Co.'s Works to form an Association team, and on Saturday they raised .a fairly good eleven and journeyed to Earlestown to play their first game, which was decided under very favourable condi- tions. For a while it was evenly contested, but he home team proved the better marksmen and *held a lead at the interval by four clear goals.

The . second half was very similarly contested, the visitors being finally outclassed by seven clear •

WARRINGTON CENTRAL v. EEAMONT.

The above clubs opened their fixtures with a friendly on Saturday, on the Steel-street ground. Both clubs were fairly represented. Beamont won the choice of ends and played with the sun and wind at their backs. Gradually the Central put Beamont on the defensive and after five min.utes' play Horrocks opened the score for them and a few minutes later the same playir added a second goal. A little later Horrocks outwitted the (lefence and completed the "hat trick." Beamont IIDW set up a strong attack and were awarded a penalty kick, from which Oxley scored, and Horrocks scored again for the Central immediately after. Hall-time: Central, 4; Beamont, 1. CROSFIELD'S JUNIORS v. GRAPPENHALL JUNIORS.

Played at Crosfield's Recreation Ground on Satur- day. Crosfield's won the toss, and Grappenhall set the ball rolling. From a mistake on the part of Hardy, the home full-back, the visitors scored. The home team then took the offensive, and from a fine centre by Bissell, Sudlow scored. Half-time arrived with the score even. The second half was well contested, and both teams failed to score again. Result: Crosfield's, 1 goal; Grappehhall, 1 goal.' For the home team Beswick, Mason and Bissell played a good game, while for the visitors Barton played excellently in goal.

LING.

THREE-ASIDE GAMES.,

Works Association Competition

Concluded.

The postponed three-aside bowling competition in connection with the Warrington Works Amateur Association was brought to a conclusion on the Arp- ley green on Saturday. Beautiful weather condi- tions prevailed, and the sport was of the best. The final game between Mr. T: Mannering's team (No. 1) and Mr- H. Whitley's team resulted in a draw, following which a deciding game of five up was played, Mr, liannering's team winning by 15 points to 8.

LADY JELLICOE.

Father's Death Prevents Visit

To Warrington.

GENERAL REGRET AND SYMPATHY.

Last week the town was thrilled by the an- nouncement that Lady Jellicoe was to visit Warrington yesterday in order to receive at the hands of the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) in the Parr Hall, the cheque for £2,160 raised at the Navy Day held in August for the benefit of Admiral jellicoe's fund. However, owing to the illness of her father, Sir Charles Caynr, Lady Jellicoe telegraphed on Tuesday cancel- ling the engagement, and her fears for his con- dition had melancholy confirmation on Thurs- day, when he passed away at his residence, Aberfoyle, Ayrshire. These announcements have caused widespread disappointment i regret and sympathy—dis- appointment at not being able to afford her ladyship hospitality, regret that her father should have been removed by death from a sphere of usefulness, and sympathy with her and the family in their bereavement. Expres- sive of these sentiments the Mayor (Alderiopan Peter Peacock), with his usual promptitude and consideration, hal. written Lady Jellicoe a letter on the town's behalf.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
Children See The Big Chcque.

News of her Ladyship's inability to be pre- sent was first received by telegram on Tuesday afternoon, and the Mayor immediately went t..) the Parr Hall .to acquaint the ,school children rehearsing for the demonstration arranged for last night. Their sorrow was generally mani- fest. Partly with the idea of mitigating it the Mayor showed them the big cheque which was to have been presented. It is drawn on the Warrington Branch of the Manchester and Liverpool District Banking Company, being made out by the Mayor to Admiral Sir John Jellicoe and "crossed." Its signatories are Harold Smith, president; Peter Peacock (Mayor), chairman and hon. treasurer; Nora Rylands, hon. secretary for the Warrington Navy Fund. The children having looked at it most interestingly-it was the biggest cheque they had ever seen—the Mayor placed it in hic-• wallet, and, returning to the Town Hall, put it safely away until further arrangements have been made for its presentation. When and how this will take place has not yet been decided, suggestions from Lady Jellicoe being awaited.

MILITARY TRIBUNALS.

CLASS "W."

Men Who Cannot Be "Placed"

By Medical Board.

Wednesday's Tribunal in the Town Hall was composed of Dr. Joseph (Deputy Mayor) in the chair, Alderman Tinnion (who subsequently presided), Mr. F. A. Frost and Mr. W. Brad- bury. Captain Hackett attended as military repre- gentative, Captain Mathams being at present on sick leave.

The ingenuity of the Medical Board in classify- ing the fit and partially fit has apparently added a further class to their many differentia- tions.

The Class is lettered "W," but what thb letter stands for is a mystery. Its meaning was made clear by the Military Representa- tive. It signifies the case of a man who is not utterly rejected by the Medical Board, and who is allowed to continue in civil life. He is not exactly discharged; and is still in the Army Reserve.

A case maker on whose behalf application was made for exemption, came within this category, and the Tribunal had no alternative.

Cotton Mill Employe. A young man, aged 18 years, employed at the Coekbedge Factory, applied for exemption. He had three brothers 'in the Army—one of whom had been discharged disabled, and three other brothers working at munitions. His father died last year and applicant had to support his mother and sister.—The application was re- fused, Alderman Tinnion remarking that the Tribunal thought a couple of the brothers could contribute to the home. Workhouse Porter. .The Warrington Board of Guardians appealed on behalf of the WorkhouSe porter, a married man aged 38 years, whose wife is also in the service of the Board. _ .

' Mr. Bottomley, clerk to the Guardians, stated ' that while the man is porter at the Workhouse and deals with "casuals," &c., he has other duties to perform. There is 'a military hospital iwith over 300 patients, and whenever any of these were going in or coming out of the hos- pital every day, they had to be booked. Ther. were only three men of military age in the • institution, one of whom was exempt. There were also some dangerous lunatics to deal with from time to time, and the Guardians found it very difficult to get an experienced man. ' The Chairman: Suppose the Tribunal are against you what will you do? ' Mr. Bottomley: We will have to do best we can. He further mentioned that the man had , only been passed for garrison duty. They would not have appealed at all if he had been passed for general service. The porter said it was not his wish to come before the Tribunal. He would prefer to join the Army, but the Guardians thought his ser- vices more useful there. • Mr. Bottomley, in reply to the Chairman, said the man's, wages would be made up. A month's exemption, was granted. • Exempted On Medical Grounds.

A gas fitter employed by a public body appealed for exemSion on domestic grounds, and for medical reasons. He handed in a doctor's certificate to the Tribunal and re- marked that he had been passed on August 19th for Class A, general service. *The Chairman: liaa you that complaint at the time you were examined? Applieant: Yes; but I had been previously rejected. The Chairman: Did they see that certificate? Applicant: They examined me generally. Mr. Holloway: Was the examination so general that it was not particular? (Laughter.) Applicant: I don't know: it was not very long. He added that his medical man told him he would hare to undergo an operation for the complaint. Exemption was granted on medical grounds.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
One of Two Brothers to Go.

A furniture dealer and draper having two shops in Market-street, aged 37 years, married, applied for exemption. His brother is in part- nership with him, is 33 years, unmarried, and has been exempted. His exemption exnires in October.—The Chairman, in stating that the edse would be ailjourned for a week, said that applicant's brother, who had got exemption. was a single roan, alkl the, Tribunal were of opinion that the business -ould be managed by one of them. One would have to go, and the ease would be adjourned for a week in order to decide which should go.

Nineteen-year-old Farm Hand.

A farmer who knew that hi t 4 land is within the borough because he pays Corpotation rates, created much laughter by making the an- nouncement. But when he asked the Tribunal to exempt a teamsman nineteen years of age, from military service, the Court assumed an extremely serious aspect.—The application wa3 refused. Class " Bl."

A' cabinetmaker, 34„ married, classed by the Medical Board as "El," and whose father told the Tribunal that he suffers from, chronic rheumatism, was given conditional exemption. He has two brothers in' the Army.—When asked M the man would be willing to join the V.T.C.. his father said that be was not only a Corporal in that body, but a. member of the Voluntary Fire Brigade.

MORE INDISPENSABLES.

Business Men's Pleas For Employes.

Dr. Joseph (Deputy-Mayor), Alderman Tinnion, Messrs. W. Bradbury, F. A. Frost and J. Elwell. sat on Thursday afternoon at the Town Hall.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
Butchers' Appeals.

A wholesale butcher appealed on behalf of his head slaughterman, aged 23% years, and unmarried. His average kill every week was 50 beasts and 250 sheep and there- was only this man besides himself and a shopman. He could not get a slaughtermaii.—The Chairman said the Tribunal would consider their deci- sion and let him know.

Another master butcher, doing an extensive business in Warrington'and Latchford, applied for exemption for two of his employes, who, in addition to working in the slaughter-house, look after branch establishments.—The Chair- man said the Tribunal thought that one of these men should go and would consider what arrangements could be made. Applicant would hear in due course what the arrangements were.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
Studying Wireless.

A young man aged 22 years, employed by Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., stated in his appeal that he had only been passed for Section 2 of Class B—" Work in labour units or on garrison duty. or regimental out- door employment." Re was studying wireless telegraphy at Liverpool and his first examina- tion would come off in December and the final next February.—The Tribunal agreed to give him temporary exemption if he continued his studies, and when he had passed his examina- tions his services might be required in some department where he would be useful to the country.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
Machine Repairer Exempted.

A. firm of velvet, cord and fustian manufac- turers, who were represented by Mr. Arthur Browne; solicitor, applied on behalf of a cutter and machine repairer, a married man, aged 35 years, Whose services were described, as in- dispensable. They have only one other male employe, and the .head • of the firm has two sons in the Army.—The Tribunal granted condi- tional exemption with the V.T.C. proviso.

MARKET GARDENER'S COMPLAINT.

Conccientious Objector Gets His

Job.

Mr. George Mather presided at Wednesday's meeting of the Warrington Rural Military Tribunal.

Before going into the list the Chairman read the following characteristic letter, received from a. Padgate market gardener': "Dear Sir,— I would like to mention a few facts as regards myself as being 'called upon on Monday next, 2nd October. When my brother appealed for me he appealed on behalf of me as a market gardener and as a tomato, cucumber and vege- table grower, I only got six weeks' exemption, final. There is- another man .who appealed at the other local Tribunal on conscientious grounds got off on condition that he gets work on a farm or market garden work: that is the only thing he can do that is of national im- portance; and this same 'man.has got my situa- tion, which I don't think is fair, and which I think the Tribunal will agree with me. I think a man who has been used to gardening and farm work ail! his life is of more national im- portanee than a man who has never done any before. It seems as if I have to go out and make room for a conscientious man, who is about- the same age as myself. He has only two children and I have five, and I am sorry 4 o say my wife is expecting the sixth shortly, and my wife is of a nervous disposition. I think. that if the Tribunal will consider .these facts they will agree with me that I would .be of more national importance, at home than the man, who has taken my situation. I think I have only done right in letting you know these facts."

The Chairman said this man was before theta six or. ,eight weeks-ago—his brother appealed for him. The Tribunal said he Must go, and now it appeared that a man who had been be- fore another Tribunal and pleaded 'conseienze was told to find work on a farm and got this

raan'4 place. , A. M. Mitchell: If it is as he states it is a most -unfair case. _ •

Another Member: Perhaps the brother has taken this man on on purpose to make the case stronger. Mr. Ball asked if they were going to counter. balance their deeds and actions by th'e deeds and actions of another Tribunal.

The ,Chairman: It is, in my opinion, far from reasonable.

Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Surely a man with five children has no right to be sent out when a man with two children gets his place Mr. Backhouse said they had not seen the, certificate which was granted in the other man's case.

Mr. Furness (military representative) said that a number of conscientious objectors were being dealt with by the Tribunal in Lo4don and given the option of going to farming work. , The Tribunal ultimately agreed that they could not interfere, and while expressing sym- pathy with the applicant, decided that the matter must take AS course.

MEN'S WAGES FOR BOYS.

Tanning Company's Appeal To

Runcorn Rural Tribunal.

An interesting statement with regard to the wages paid to munition workers was made at a meeting of the Runcorn Rural Tribunal, at the Workhouse, Dutton, on Thursday. Mr. C. E. Linaker, sen., presided. At the last sitting it was decided that all men aged 25 and under employed by the AStmbor Tanning Company should. present themselves for examination by, the Medical Board. Mr. Linaker, solicitor, now gave the .result of the examination. Four men had been - passed for Class A, one for Class C, and three for Class C (iii.).

Mr. Moors, the works' manager, said that he employed boys instead of women. Mr. Antwis : What wages do you pay to boys?

Mr. Moors reulied that boys of 16 were earn- ing from 198. 6d. to 255. per week, and youths of 18 from 425. to-455.

Mr. Linaker submitted that the company had already released as many men as possible. Five of the men were exempted urrtil the Ist November, without leave to appeal, and the rest of the cases, numbering 34, were ad- journed for two months. Could He Get His Knee Up?

Ekemption was sought on behalf of a Preston Brook teamsman,, whose employer put forward the view that he was of greater use in agri- cultural work than he would be in the Army. Exemption until Ist January.—The Chairman: Is he a member of the Volunteer Training Corps?—The Employer: No.—The Chairman: Well, then, he must join.—The Employer: I'm afraid he won't get his knee up very well after sowing wiieat.

In Charge of Burial Ground.

In the case of a sexton, caretaker of a chapel and insurance agent, living at Appleton, it was stated that the appellant had charge of a Nonconformist Burial Ground. It was the only Nonconformist Burial Ground just out- side Warrington and served a considerable area. Appellant, who was 35, had six children.— The appeal was disallowed.

A Stockton Heath One-Man Business. Stockton Heath fish, fruit and vegetable salesman applied for exemption, through Mr. H. J. Westbrook, solicitor, Warrington. The man had been temporarily exempted to Ist September. He was 39 years of apt and had a family of two. The Medical Board lad passed him for garrison duty or in provisional units. He had no one to assist except his own son, aged 15. He put in 14 hours each day (except Sunday) and on Saturdays 18 hours. He did all the buying and his own carting and had to cut the growing vegetables and load them him- self.

Mr. Westbrook supported the application by a personal appeal, saying he had known appli- cant all his life and could testify to the hard work and industry by which he had built up his small business. If ever there was a case of a one-man business which deserved consider- ation this was such a case. The applicant was a member of the Stockton Heath V.T.C.

The Tribunal granted temporary exemption to Ist January, 1917.

===== 0002601_19160930_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
A 25% INCREASE.

Bold Street Wesleyans Aim At Development.

The Rev. Percy Meson has been pastor of Bold-street Wesleyan Church for a year, and to celebrate the fact and propagate a new scheme of development, special services were held on Thursday. At that in the evening Mr. meson thanked the people for all they had done for him, and added that although it had been a year full of difficulties for them all, they had reason for profound thanks. During the past few days there had been growing in their midst a spirit of hope and enthusiasm, and they had not been depressed. With so many away from home upon whom they could have relied they might have let ' things slide, but the workers at that church had not taken up that position, but had decided to do something more than they had yet achieved. They had pledged themselves that in every department associated with the church there should be a 25 per cent. increase by next year. Every department had met and discussed, the means by which it was to he done, and some of them had already had sub- stantial increases. The Sunday school, the Wesley Guild, the Band of Hope, and those in charge of the classes attached to the church were all concerned. On Thursday of last ‘week a united meeting of all the workers was held and they definitely committed themselves to their programme and definitely yielded them- selves to Jesus Christ. It would not be easy; it was not going to be a case of a sudden dash and mighty success, but a constant, steady effort, securing the ground as they were going for- ward and making success absolutely certain. Mr. J. G. Withinshaw, who presided, re- marked that there was nothing more difficult to move than a Church which was only half alive. Like a business, any Church which was to go ahead had to be moved from the top. If the head of a business were vigorous and enter- prising his employes were usually so; but if he were slack they were slack, too. At Bald- street they were fortunate in having an earnest and energetic pastor and a good band of workers, and he wished them every success

with their ambitious scheme. Bold-street had a great name and, no doubt, they would live up to it.

-Other speakers were the Rev. W. T. Derry (superintendent of the circuit) and the Rev. T. A. Turney, of Liverpool. The last-named ,+lqo preached during the afternoon.

TO REDUCE BIG DEBT.

Sale Of Work Held At Brick

Street Mission.

To reduce a church mortgage debt of £l,OOO, a sale of work was held in the Brick-street Mission on Thursday and Friday. The opening ceremony on Thursday was per- formed by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), and with his Worship on the platform were Councillor Henshall (chairman), Councillor Shaw and Mr. William Barton (president of the Church). .Referring to the sale .of work, the Chairman said they hoped to raise £2OO as a result of that effort. The workers had laboured very hard, and were very deserving. (Applause.) The Mayor said it was quite true that this was his first public appearance since he bad been selected Mayor for the fourth year. If a salary had been attached to his responsible position he would have refused it, but he felt that baying gone through many of the troublesi, &c., brought about by the war, he thought he might be one of the fittest persons in Warring- ton to extend. the hand of help and sympathy to those cases which came under his notice. (Hear, hear.) Continuing, the Mayor said Ile was always willing to help anything in con- nectfon with the Church, and if all the people of Warrington would do the same' he would be perfectly satisfied. If there was ever a time when the Church was badly needed, it was the present time. In conclusion, the Mayor apologised for the absence of the Mayoress, wh6 had gone to Wales. .

The takings at Thursday's proceedings were about £9O.

FRIDAY'S OPENING CEREMONY.

Councillor J. T. Locker performed the open- ing ckremony yesterday, Mr.' James Hudson presiding. Councillor Locker said it 'must lie very encouraging to the workers to find they had been able to support themselves so well. He had been conversant with their church for a great number of years, and had watched its pro- gress with pleasure and pride. He was pleased 'to hear they had done 60 well the previous day, and hoped they would do better still that day. (Applause.) Mrs. Thos. Smith proposed and Mrs. W. Barton, sen., seconded a vote of thanks TO Councillor Locker for opening the sale and to Mr. Hudson for presiding: A buttonhole was presented to Councillor Locker and Mr. Hudson by Miss A. Bibby. Entertainments and competitions were held each evening.

PRIVATE .11. SMART. Third Brother Who Has Been Wounded. Private Harold Sinart, South Lancs. Regi- ment, son of Mr. Smart, confectioner, 32, Queen-street, has been wounded. This is the third and youngest of Mr. Smart's sons to he wounded. Private Smart is progressing as well as can be expected. He is 22 years of age, and

hind they will get one of our rifles and fire cn us as soon as we turn our backs." Rifleman Howard, who is nineteen years of age, joined the Army in August, 1914, and went to the Front in May last year. He was wounded the following August, and after four; months in an, English hospital, he went back in November. He was educated at the Wycliffe and Beamont Schools, and was formerly employed by McDowells, motor builders. He belonged to the Ist Warrington Troop Boy Scouts, and formerly attended Orford Sunday School. - PRIVATE J. WAKEFIELD. Gassed In June Last Year; Nov Wounded. these with cart-grease or any other sticky sub- stance. On no account paint the stem of the tree. Examine the bands frequently, and if it is found that the grease or whatever is used Is in the least dry, smear the bands a second time. If any apples have fallen owing to the attack of the Codlin moth, all such should be gathered up .and put on the fire before the caterpillars have had time to escape from them. \ THE FRUIT CROPS. From what I have seen, ,and the reports of friends, the fruit.,crop is a very poor one, henev it will be necessary to be careful of what we have, the keeping largely depending on the gathering and the mode of storing. A very important point is the right time at which to gather the various sorts. Many people gather much too soon, with the result that the fruits shrivel,and are of no value. Choose a dry day for gathering,- and handle the fruits very care- fully so as not to bruise them in any way. Never lay ;he fruits on straw or hay, as 'these impart a bad' flavour to them and spoil them. The later the season of the apple the longer should it remain on the tree. Frost will not injure the fruit, but the heavy gales ,that" we often get at this season play havoc with it, and to guard against loss in this way one is tempted prematurely to gather the fruit. This applies both to apples and pears.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."

Deaths. DENTON.—Died of wounds. Private J. Denton. dearly beloved and second son of the late Matter- son Denton and Elizabeth Denton, 21, Dixon- street, Warrington, aged 23 years. Sadly missed by all at home. A good life is too short lived, but a good name endureth for ever. In Memoriam. HARDMAN.—In loving memory of Rifleman John Hardman, of the Rifle Brigade, who was killed by a sniper in France on September 24th, 1915. Sleep on, dear son, in a foreign grave. • Your life for your country you nobly gave, No loved ones beside you to say Goo-bye, But safe in God's keeping now you he. "Duty nobly done." Deeply mourned by MOTHER. SISTERS, and only BROTHER. 47, Scott-street, Warrington. HOWELL.—In loving memory of our dear Mothei, , who passed away October Ist. 1913. oETs flee &JAsCwlisy,,.. One long, sad year has passed away, Since our great sorrow fell; Yet in our hearts we mourn the loss Of him we loved so well. . FATHER. PRIVATE P. L. ROWE. Killed.

much regret to have to tell you that Private P. L. Rowe, 2nd Grenadier. Guards, died at 1.45 a.m. on the 22nd inst. He was brought to hos- pital very severely wounded in the body, and from the first there was very little hope of sav- ing his life. He was buried in the cemetery at- tached to the hospital." Private Rowe, who was. educated at St. Bene- dict's and St. Alban's Schools, was formerly employed by the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., but prior to the war was work- ing as a warder .at the Haydock Lodge Private Asylum. Enlisting in September, 1914, he had been in France 14 months. He is 22 years of age. His father was a school attenclance.officer at Widnes, and served for over 12 years with the Somerset Light Infantry. Corporal John Rowe, his brother, is serving with the Man- chester Regiment. Another brother is .. Sergeant-Major in a Volunteer Training Corps. PRIVATE J. STEWART. Missing Since Loos ; Now Reported Killed. Private John Stewart, Royal Scots Regiment, husband of Mrs. Stewart and father of six chil- dren, who reside at 16, Derby-street, having been missing since the battle of Loos in 1915, the War Office have now. notified Mrs. Stewart that they must regretfully conclude that he has died as no further news has been heard of him. Private Stewart last wrote home on September 24th, 1915, to tell his wife that the British were "going to make things hum," and that She must "look out for developments." He was a native

PRIVATE E. PYE Killed.

Mrs. Heesom,, on September sth, said, "I have' sad news for - you about your dear brother, Ernest Pye. He was killed in action on the 29th August, and died without any pain. Well liked amongst his chums, he was a good chap." The cqmrade adds that a parcel of cigarettes belonging to the dead /Private was opened and distributed among his pals. Private Pye himself wrote home about a week prior to his death. After acknowledging the receipt of a; letter and some "fags," he said he was in the best of health, and in a wood near the trenches. "I am," he proceeds, "getting about sick of this weary life, but we have to make the best of it and hope mr better days to come." Private Pye, who enlisted on the outbreak of war, and had been in France twelve months, was formerly in the band at the Ragged School. Prior to enlisting he was employed by Messrs. Rylands, Bros.. Ltd., and was educated at Silver- street School. He was in his '2oth year. Private Pye's brother-in-law, Rifleman John Heesom, in the King's Royal Rifles, has been in France for 10 months.

the Front ten weeks. He was educated at Heathside School and worshipped at St. Luke's Church and attended the school. A younger brother, Edward, is serving abroad with the R.F.A., having been in the Army since the outbreak of war.

SERGT. BRADBURY'S CONDITION. Mr. William Bradbury, a m?mber of the War_ rington Military Tribunal, has been to see his son, Sergeant Bradbury, at the Northampton War Hospital. Although severely wounded in the stomach—the intestines being perforated in no less than nineteen, places—every hope Is entertained by the medical staff for thair patient's ultimate recovery. It is hoped that this gallant young fellow will soon be restored to health. LIEUT. S. J. PRICE. Resigns Commission Owing To 11l- Health. Many people in Warrington will hear with regret that Quartermaster and Hon. Lieutenant S. J. Price, of the South Lancashire Regiment, whose home is in Alexandra-street, relinquished his commission on the 23rd September, owing to ill-health. Mr. Pries is a son of the late Mr. Joseph Price, who served the Colours for forty-eight years, fought in the Indian Mutiny (and for many years was superior barrack sergeant at Orford. Barracks. He joined the South Lanca- shire Regiment in September, nit, and gained rapid promotion. He was gazetted honorary lieutenant and quartermaster on November 6th,

The Liberal Club held their closing main on Saturday, when nearly fifty- members took part. This is a record for these times. There was some good bowling, although the green was slightly damp, and the interest the members took was high. Many thanks are due to the gentlemen who gave the prizes. The successful members were as follow: 1, George Marshall (hon. secretary); 2, Frank Stir- lingileet; 3, T. Drew; 4, John Collins; 5. Frank Plurapton; 6, William Daintith; 7, Thomas Dutton: 8, •John Graham. The prizes will be distributed by Alderman D. Tinnion on the general meeting night in October. LIBERAL CI.US v.
